UNITED STATES CLAIMS COURT 


Civil Action No. 154-90-8-C 


WINSTAR CORPORATION, 
1711 West County Road B 
Suite 300 South 
Roseville, Minnesota 55113, 


and 


UNITED FEDERAL SAVINGS BANK 
1715 West County Road B2 
Roseville, Minnesota 55113, Plaintiffs, 


V. 


THE UNiTEeD States, Defendant. 


COMPLAINT 


For its Complaint against the United States 
(hereinafter sometimes referred to as the “gov- 
ernment”), the plaintiffs Winstar Corporation 
(“Winstar”) and United Federal Savings Bank 
(“United”) state as follows: 

1. Winstar is a Minnesota corporation organized 
in 1984 for the purpose of acquiring an insolvent 
savings and loan institution, Windom Federal 


7 


Savings and Loan Association (“Windom”), in a 
merger induced by the Federal Savings and Loan 
Insurance Corporation (“FSLIC”) and the Federal 
Home Loan Bank Board (“FHLBB”) pursuant to 
thei statutory and supervisory authority over fed- 
erally insured savings and loan institutions (hereinaf- 
ter referred to as “thrifts” or “thrift institutions”). 

2. United is a Minnesota corporation, all of the 
stock of which is owned by Winstar. United was 
formed in 1984 for the purpose of acquiring Windom, 
which was merged into United that year. 

3. As shown in more detail below, Winstar forn.ed 
United and acquired Windom pursuant to Winstar - 
and United’s contract with the government, which 
obviated Windom’s liquidation by FSLIC and 


_FHLBB and, consequently, saved FSLIC’s insurance 


fund millions of dollars that it would have been 
obligated to pay to creditors and depositors of 
Windom had FSLIC and FHLBB been forced to 
liquidate it. Winstar and United have fully performed 
their obligations under their contract with the United 
States. Although the United States has received all of 
the benefits of its contract with Winstar and United, 
the United States has breached its contract with the 
enactment of the Financial Institutions Reform, 
Recovery, and Enforcement Act of 1989 (“FIRREA”), 
Pub. Law. No. 101-75, 103 Stat. 183. The United 
States’ breach of its contract with Winstar and 
United entirely frustrates the purpose of the con- 
tract. Additionally, FIRREA’s repudiation and abro- 
gation of the government’s contractual promises to 
Winstar and United constitute a taking of Winstar 
and United’s property without just compensation in 
violation of the Fifth Amendment to the United 
States Constitution and a deprivation of Winstar and 
United’s property without due process of law in 


8 


violation of the Fifth Amendment to the United 
States Constitution. 


JURISDICTION 


4. This court has jurisdiction over the subject 
matter of this action pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§ 1491(a)(1). 


BACKGROUND 


5. During the late 1970’s and early 1980’s unprece- 
dented high interest rates pushed the thrift indus- 
try’s cost of retaining and obtaining new deposits 
substantially above the rate of return on long-term, 
fixed-rate mortgages in thrift portfolios, precipitating 
a financial crisis in the thrift industry. By mid-1981, 
the thrift industry’s cost of funds had risen to 10.31 
percent, substantially exceeding the 9.75 percent 
average return on thrift mortgage portfolios. This 
“negative spread” widened in 1982. 

6. In order to present massive withdrawals by 
depositors, thrift institutions were forced to pay 
higher rates on savings accounts. Savers nonetheless 
withdrew $31.8 billion more than they deposited in 
1981-1982. 

7. As a result of the substantial negative spread 
between the thrift industry’s cost of funds and its 
return on assets, and the large net withdrawal of 
deposits, the thrift industry sustained an operating 
loss of $8.9 billion in 1981-1982, with a record $3.3 
billion loss in the first half of 1982 alone. During that 
period the thrift industry’s net worth declined more 
than $7 billion. In March 1982 Richard T. Pratt, then 


9 


Chairman of FHLBB, advised a congressional com- 
mittee: “(T]he thrift industry in the United States is 
undergoing a severe financial crisis ... [H]igh and 
volatile interest rates ... are literally threatening the 
survival of this industry....” 

8. Many FSLIC-insured thrift institutions either 
became insolvent or were threatened with insolvency 
in the early 1980’s, and FSLIC’s insurance fund was 
faced with the potential loss of many billions of 
dollars in claims by depositors of these insolvent or 
troubled thrifts. For example, during the last half of 
1981 and the first half of 1982, more than 80 percent 
of all FSLIC-insured thrift institutions incurred 
operating losses. As of the end of 1981, almost 10 
percent of FSLIC-insured thrift institutions had a net 


worth of less than 2 percent of their total assets. 


Three hundred fifty-six (356) FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions with $72 billion in assets fell into this 
category. 

9. Under the National Housing Act of 1934, as 
amended, FSLIC was at all times relevant to this 
action authorized to facilitate, upon terms and 
conditions prescribed by FSLIC, the merger of a 
failing FSLI1C-insured thrift institution into a finan- 
cially sound FSLIC-insured thrift institution by 
providing financial assistance in the form of prom- 
issory notes (“FSLIC notes”) or cash contributions to 
the healthy institution or by guaranteeing it against 
loss by reason of the merger, so long as the cost of 
the financial assistance provided by FSLIC did not 
exceed the expense that would have been incurred in 
liquidating the institution and paying insurance claims 
upon its accounts. 

10. Faced with a large number of failing thrift 
institutions, FHLBB adopted and aggressively imple- 
mented a policy of requiring insolvent or troubled 


10 


thrift institutions to merge into financially sound 
acquired institutions. With respect to many such 
“supervisory” mergers, FSLIC exercised its au- 
thority under the National Housing Act to provide 
assistance in the form of cash contributions, FSLIC 
notes, and/or guarantees to induce financially sound 
acquired institutions to merge with insolvent or 
troubled thrifts. 

11. During the first 5 months of 1981, FSLIC 
provided financial assistance in 7 supervisory mer- 
gers. These 7 troubled FSLIC-insured thrifts had 
assets totaling $1.5 billion. FSLIC provided assist- 
ance in the form of cash contributions, FSLIC notes, 
and asset purchases at a total cost (at present value) 
of $344 million. FSLIC’s present value cost of 
arranging these supervisory mergers was thus 22 
percent of the total assets of the troubled thrifts, a 
typical cost-asset ratio for FSLIC-assisted super- 
visory mergers over the previous several years. 
Measured another way, the cost of arranging these 
supervisory mergers was 75 percent of the cost of 
liquidating the 7 trouble thrifts, also a typical ratio 
for FSLIC-assisted supervisory mergers. The finan- 
cial assistance provided by FSLIC in these super- 
visory mergers represented a cash outflow of about 
200 percent of the cash inflow to FSLIC during the 
5-month period. In May 1981 FSLIC ran out of cash 
and was forced to sell at a discount over $150 million 
from its long-term bond portfolio to fund its current 
operations. 

12. As the crisis in the thrift industry continued to 
worsen, and more and more FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions became insolvent or troubled, FSLIC and 
FHLBB recognized that continuing to facilitate 
supervisory mergers with cash contributions and 
FSLIC notes would quickly and entirely exhaust the 


11 


insurance fund, which at the end of 1981 had total 
assets of $7 billion. Indeed, during the last 7 months 
of 1981, FSLIC provided financial assistance in 
connection with 23 supervisory mergers. The 23 failed 
thrift institutions had assets of $12.3 billion. If FSLIC 
had provided cash contributions and FSLIC notes at 
historic levels in order to induce financially healthy 
acquirer institutions to enter into these supervisory 
mergers, the cost to FSLIC would have been 
approximately $2.7 billion. Similarly, in 1982 FSLIC 
provided financial assistance in connection with 49 
supervisory transactions (44 FSLIC-assisted mergers 
and 5 FSLIC-assisted holding company acquisitions), 
involving 77 insolvent or failing thrift institutions 
with assets totaling $31.2 billion. If FSLIC had 
provided cash contributions and FSLIC notes at 
historic levels in connection with these supervisory 
mergers, the cost to FSLIC would have been at least 
$6.9 billion. According to FSLIC’s own calculations, 
providing financial assistance at historic levels would 
have exhausted the FSLIC insurance fund by April of 
1982. 

13. In mid-1981, FSLIC and FHLBB were thus 
forced to revise their policies and practices regarding 
the provision of financial assistance in supervisory 
mergers in order to prevent, or at least forestall, 
exhaustion of the FSLIC insurance fund. As de- 
scribed below, because FSLIC and FHLBB could no 
longer provide the amount of cash or other tangible 
financial assistance necessary to induce healthy 
acquirer institutions to merge with insolvent or 
troubled FSLIC-insured thrifts, FSLIC and FHLBB 
began inducing and arranging supervisory mergers by 
providing assistance in the form of an intangible 
asset—“supervisory goodwill.” FSLIC and FHLBB 
also expanded the number of potential acquirers of 


12 


insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured thrift institu- 
tions by permitting interstate mergers (in the 
absence of an acceptable merger proposal from a 
financially sound in-state acquirer institution), and 
inter-industry mergers (in the absence of an accept- 
able merger proposal from either an in-state or out- 
of-state thrift institution). In evaluating merger 
proposals, FSLIC’s principal objective was to merge 
the insolvent or troubled thrift institution into a 
healthy acquirer at the lowest possible cost to 
FSLIC. As H. Brent Beesley, then Director of 
FSLIC, stated in April 1982: “Our criterion used in 
evaluating our alternatives is simple: Which alterna- 
tive represents the least cost to the FSLIC and the 
public—all things considered?” 

14. In order to induce financially sound acquirer 
institutions to merge with insolvent or failing FSLIC- 
insured thrifts with little or no cash contributions, 
FSLIC notes, or other tangible financial assistance 
‘rom FSLIC, FSLIC and FHLBB relied primarily 
upon an aggressive application of the “purchase 
method” of accounting for the mergers. The Generally 
Accepted Accounting Principles (“GAAP”) applicable 
at the time provided that in mergers and acquisitions 
using the purchase method of accounting, the book 
value of the assets and liabilities of an acquired thrift 
institution should be adjusted (“marked to market”) 
to fair market value at the time of the acquisition, 
and that any excess in the cost of the acquisition 
(which includes any liabilities assumed by the ace 
quirer) over the fair market value of the acquirer 
assets should be separately recorded on the acquirer 
institution’s books as “goodwill”—an intangible, non- 
earning asset that may be amortized on a straight- 
line basis over a period of up to 40 years. 

15. Because of the high interest rates prevailing at 
the time, the fair market value of the lower yielding 


13 


fixed-rate mortgage loans held by most acquired 
institutions was significantly less than the mortgage 
loans’ book value (the face amount of the loans). 
Accordingly, in the typical supervisory merger at that 
time, the aggregate fair market value of the liabilities 
assumed by the acquirer institution exceeded the 
aggregate fair market value of the acquired institu- 
tion’s fixed-rate loans and other assets. The amount 
by which the acquired institution’s liabilities exceed 
its assets is reflected on the merged institution’s 
books as goodwill. Goodwill recorded in connection 
with a supervisory merger is known as “supervisory 
goodwill.” 

16. Supervisory goodwill was recorded on the 
merged institution’s balance sheet as an asset 
includable in capital under GAAP and under 
FHLBB’s regulations governing the minimum capital 
requirements of FSLIC-insured thrift institutions. 
The ability to include supervisory goodwill as capital 
for purposes of satisfying FHLBB’s minimum capital 
requirements and to amortize it over a period of up to 
35 years was essential to inducing healthy acquirer 
institutions to merge with insolvent or troubled 
FSLIC-insured thrifts. By assuring acquirer institu- 
tions in supervisory mergers that supervisory good- 
will would be treated in the above-described manner 
for regulatory purposes, FSLIC and FHLBB were 
able to induce and arrange supervisory mergers with 
minimal cash contributions and other tangible finan- 
cial assistance. FSLIC and FHLBB thus saved many 
billions of dollars in financial assistance that FSLIC 
would otherwise have had to pay in order to resolve 
scores of insolvent and troubled FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions. 

17. As part of its effort to induce and arrange 
supervisory mergers by using supervisory goodwill in 


14 


the above-described manner, FHLBB took steps to 
assure the thrift industry that GAAP would control in 
determining the application of purchase method 
accounting and the treatment of supervisory goodwill 
in supervisory mergers. In August 1981, FHLBB 
withdrew a proposed rule change that would have 
imposed regulatory standards more stringent than 
GAAP for accounting for supervisory goodwill. The 
proposed rule would have limited the inclusion of 
supervisory goodwill in regulatory capital to 30 
percent of the merged institution’s net worth and 
would have required that supervisory goodwill be 
amortized over a period no longer than ten years. In 
withdrawing the proposed rule, the FHLBB stated 
that a principal reason for its action was that the 
proposal could “discourage potential merger partners 
from consummating necessary mergers.” In Septem- 
ber 1981 FHLBB issued Memorandum R31b, which 
made clear that, as a matter of FHLBB policy, GAAP 
would control in determining the application of 
purchase method accounting and the treatment of 
supervisory goodwill in supervisory mergers. 

18. During the last 7 months of 1981, FSLIC 
provided tangible financial assistance (e.g. cash, 
FSLIC notes) in connection with the merger of 23 
insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured thrift institu- 
tions with assets totaling $12.3 billion. These 23 
supervisory mergers were arranged at a present 
value cost to FSLIC of $663 million, or 5.1 percent of 
assets—approximately one-fourth of the historical 
cost-asset ratio of FSLIC-assisted supervisory mer- 
gers. Measured as a percentage of the cost of 
liquidation, the present value cost of these 23 cases 
was only 18 percent, down from historical levels of 
65-75 percent. Similarly, in 1982 FSLIC provided 
financial assistance in connection with 49 supervisory 


15 


mergers involving 77 FSLIC-insured insolvent or 
troubled thrifts with assets totaling $31.2 billion. The 
present value cost to FSLIC of these supervisory 
mergers was $1.2 billion, or 3.8 percent of assets. As 
a percent of the cost of liquidation, this present value 
cost was 16.3 percent. During this 19-month period, if 
FSLIC had been required to provide cash and other 
tangible financial assistance at historic levels in order 
to induce and arrange these supervisory mergers, the 
cost to FSLIC would have been approximately $9.6 
billion. The cost to FSLIC would have been higher 
still if FSLIC had been forced to liquidate the 
acquired thrifts. The substantial reductions in costs to 
FSLIC of financial assistance in FSLIC-assisted 
supervisory mergers during this 19-month period 
were due in large part to FSLIC’s use of supervisory 
goodwill through purchase method accounting. As 
then-Chairman Pratt stated in March 1982: “(Bly 
using mergers as a supervisory tool ..., we are 
seeking to spread as much of the industry’s own net 
worth as possible over the industry’s trouble spots as 
a means of conserving FSLIC’s insurance fund.” In 
effect, by using supervisory goodwill in accounting for 
supervisory mergers under the purchase method, 
FSLIC transferred most of its cost of resolving 
insolvent or failing thrifts to the healthy members of 
the industry and their shareholders. 

19. Many acquirer institutions were induced by 
FSLIC and FHLBB to merge with insolvent or 
failing FSLIC-insured institutions with no tangible 
financial assistance from FSLIC, but only super- 
visory goodwill. In 1981 FSLIC and FHLBB ar- 
ranged supervisory mergers for 58 insolvent or 
troubled FSLIC-insured thrifts with total assets of 
$3.6 billion, without providing any tangible financial 
assistance. In 1982 FSLIC and FHLBB arranged 166 


16 


supervisory mergers without providing tangible finan- 
cial assistance. Most of these unassisted supervisory 
mergers arranged by FSLIC and FHLBB in 1981 and 
1982 involved substantial amounts of supervisory 
goodwill. 

20. In 1982 the Financial Accounting Standards 

Board, which promulgates GAAP, issued Statement 
of Financial Accounting Standards No. 72 (“FASB 
72”), which amended GAAP applicable to mergers 
initiated after September 30, 1982. As applied to 
United, FASB 72 generally would require that 
goodwill be amortized over a period of approximately 
15 years. This change in GAAP did not alter 
FHLBB’s and FSLIC’s regulatory policies and prac- 
tices with regard to supervisory goodwill. For 
regulatory accounting purposes, FHLBB and FSLIC 
continued to induce healthy institutions to acquire 
failing thrifts by allowing supervisory goodwill to be 
included in capital and amortized over a period of 
time exceeding that now allowed by GAAP. 

21. The nature of the problems affecting the thrift 
industry changed in 1983-1984. After mid-1982, mar- 
ket interest rates declined, and the thrift industry’s 
“negative spread” improved. But in 1983 and 1984 
thrifts increasingly faced severe asset quality prob- 
lems. This asset quality problem threatened many 
thrift institutions that were just recovering from the 
negative spread problems of 1981-1982. Thirty-five 
percent of all FSLIC-insured thrifts lost money in the 
second half of 1983. 

22. Although economic conditions improved in 1983, 
the number of problem cases and the volume of assets 
handled by FSLIC in default prevention transactions 
continued at record level in the year. The FSLIC 
resolved 53 problem cases involving $17.6 billion in 
assets in 1983. This marked a decrease from 77 


17 


FSLIC-resolved cases in 1982, but the complexity of 
the problem cases increased significantly. FSLIC 
realized significant cost savings by resolving most of 
these problem cases through interstate and inter- 
industry acquisitions. Through aggressive use of 
purchase method accounting, FSLIC saved an esti- 
mated $2.4 billion over the cost of liquidation in 
resolving these problem cases. 

23. Overall thrift industry profitability declined in 
1984, despite the fact that interest rates were far 
below the 1981-1982 levels. In 1984 FSLIC resolved 
27 problem cases involving $5.5 billion in assets. The 
continued growth in the size and complexity of 
problem cases requiring FSLIC intervention, how- 
ever, created a record-high demand on FSLIC 


' resources, precipitating a decline in FSLIC reserves 


from $6.4 billion at the end of 1983 to approximately 
$5.6 billion at the end of 1984. During 1984, despite 
FSLIC’s efforts to obtain less costly resolutions, 
FSLIC was forced to place 9 institutions into 
liquidating receiverships. The other 18 problem cases 
were resolved by FSLIC at approximately 45 percent 
of the estimated cost of liquidation, or a savings of 
$693 million, largely through the use of supervisory 
goodwill to induce healthy institutions to take over 
the failing thrifts. One of the 27 problem cases of 
1984, resolved through the use of supervisory 
goodwill, was the FSLIC-assisted takeover of Win- 
dom Federal Savings & Loan. 


THE MERGER 
24. In late 1983 Windom faced insolvency. In March 


of that year Windom’s Board of Directors acknowl- 
edged that grounds existed for the appointment of a 


18 


conservator or receiver, and that Windom’s failure 
could not be prevented without action by FSLIC. For 
the fiscal year ended June 30, 1983, the accounting 
firm of McGladrey Hendrickson & Co. reported a net 
loss for Windom of $2,766,269.00, and concluded that 
Windom “may be unable to continue in existence.” As 
of December 31, 1983, Windom reported a net worth 
of negative $2.63 million on assets of $50.1 million. 

25. As the FHLBB stated in a Resolution approv- 
ing United’s merger with Windom, “{sJevere financial 
conditions exist which threaten the stability of 
Windom.” In 1984 FHLBB specifically found that 
“Windom is insolvent in that its assets are less than 
its obligations to creditors and others, including its 
members.” In an effort to avoid the expense of 
liquidating Windom, FHLBB began seeking finan- 
cially sound merger partners in 1983. 

26. In soliciting merger proposals, FSLIC and 
FHLBB sought to attract financially sound acquirer 
institutions with sufficient financial and managerial 
resources to arrest Windom’s further deterioration, 
and, over time, to place it on a sound financial footing. 
Winstar was such an institution. 

27. In 1983 Winstar submitted a bid for the 
acquisition of Windom, and Winstar began negotiating 
with FHLBB and FSLIC regarding its proposed 
acquisition of Windom. Throughout the entire bidding 
and negotiating process it was understood that the 
entity that acquired Windom would be able to record 
as supervisory goodwill the amount by which the 
market value of Windom’s liabilities exceeded the 
market value of its assets, and to amortize that 
goodwill over a long period of time, such as 30 to 40 
years. 

28. Under the terms negotiated by the parties, 
FSLIC promised to provide to United a cash 


19 


contribution of $1,950,000, plus a sum equal to 
Windom’s aggregate negative net worth (the amount 
by which its liabilities exceeded its assets, measured 
at book value rather than fair market value). In 
return, Winstar agreed to form United and to invest 
$2 million in cash in United through the purchase of 
all of United’s common stock. 

29. FSLIC and FHLBB determined that Winstar’s 
bid was the most attractive of all bids made for 
Windom. After FSLIC and FHLBB selected tie bid, 
Winstar formed United, which entered into a “Com- 
bination Agreement and Plan of Merger” (“Merger 
Agreement”) with Windom setting out the terms of 
the acquisition. The Merger Agreement was expressly 


_ conditioned upon FSLIC’s execution of an Assistance 


Agreement approved by FHLBB. 

30. The Merger Agreement was incorporated by 
reference into the Assistance Agreement among 
FSLIC, Winstar, and United. The Assistance Agree- 
ment expressly states that FSLIC had determined, 
pursuant to Section 406 of the National Housing Act, 
that Windom “is in danger of default and that the 
amount of [FSLIC’s financial] assistance and indem- 
nification would be less than the losses [FSLIC] 
would sustain upon the liquidation of [Windom] 
through a receivership accompanied by the payment 
of insurance of accounts.” 

31. In entering into the Assistance Agreement with 
Winstar and United, FSLIC determined that Winstar 
and United’s proposal would entail the least amount 
of tangible financial assistance from FSLIC and that 
Winstar and United possessed the financial and 
managerial resources necessary to halt Windom’s 
further decline and, over time, to restore it to 
profitability. In keeping with the terms negotiated by 
the parties, the Assistance Agreement called for 


20 


FSLIC to provide to United a cash contribution of 
$1,950,000, plus a sum equal to Windom’s aggregate 
negative net worth (the amount by which its 
liabilities exceeded its assets, measured at face value 
rather than fair market value). The Assistance 
Agreement provides that the $1,950,000 cash contri- 
bution shall be directly credited to United’s regula- 
tory net worth requirements. 

32. The Assistance Agreement provides that the 
accounting treatment for the transaction shall be 
governed by GAAP, except that where GAAP conflict 
with the Assistance Agreement, FHLBB or FSLIC 
regulations, or FHLBB or FSLIC resolutions or 
actions, then the Agreement, regulation, resolution, 
or action shall govern. 

33. The Assistance Agreement expressly referred 
to FHLBB’s Resolution No. 84-363 approving the 
supervisory merger. In that resolution, FHLBB, as 
the operating head of FSLIC, expressly determined, 
pursuant to its authority under Section 406 of the 
National Housing Act, that “(1) financial assistance by 
the FSLIC is necessary to prevent the default of 
Windom and to facilitate the Merger with [United], 
and (2) the amount of such assistance, to be provided 
in accordance with the terms and conditions of the 
proposed Assistance Agreement, would not exceed 
the amount reasonably necessary to save the cost of 
liquidating Windom through a receivership accom- 
panied by the payment of insurance of accounts....” 

34. The FHLBB Resolution recognized that the 
merger would result in goodwill, providing that 
_ United 


shall furnish an analysis, accompanied by a 
concurring option from its independent public 
accountants, satisfactory to the Supervisory 
Agent and to the Office of Examinations and 


21 


Supervisions, which (i) specifically describes as of 
the closing date, any intangible assets, including 
goodwill or the discounts and premiums arising 
from the acquisition to be recorded on [United’s] 
consolidated books; and (ii) substantiates the 
reasonableness and conformity with regulatory 
requirements of the amounts attributed to intan- 
gible assets, including goodwill, and the discounts 
and premiums and the related amortization 
periods and methods.... 


The conditions referred to in the FHLBB Resolution 
were satisfied by Winstar and United. 

35. Also as part of the inducement for the merger, 
FHLBB set forth its commitment regarding the 


_ accounting treatment to be given to supervisory 


goodwill in a separate forbearance letter to United’s 
Board of Directors. That forbearance letter confirms 
the parties’ understanding that: 


For purposes of reporting to [FHLBB], the value 
of any intangible assets resulting from accounting 
for the merger in accordance with the purchase 
method may be amortized by [United] over a 
period not to exceed 35 years by the straight-line 
method .... 


36. The forbearance letter also confirmed that 
United may book the $1,950,000 cash contribution 
from FSLIC “as a direct credit to its net worth.” 

37. The supervisory merger between United and 
Windom was consummated on July 12, 1984, in 
accordance with the terms and conditions set forth in 
the agreements described above. Under the merger 
Winstar paid out of its pocket $2 million to acquire 
Windom through its merger with United. The 
resulting institution became United. 


22 


38. Under the Assistance Agreement FSLIC con- 
tributed $1,950,000 in cash. FSLIC ultimately paid 
$3,694,499 as its contribution based on Windom’s 
aggregate negative net worth. 

39. In reliance upon FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s express 
agreements concerning the use of purchase method 
accounting, the assets and liabilities of Windom were 
adjusted to fair market value at the time of the 
supervisory merger, and the resulting excess of 
liabilities over assets was recorded on United’s books 
as supervisory goodwill to be included in capital and 
amortized on a straight-line basis over a period of 35 
years. The amount of supervisory goodwill ultimately 
recorded by United as a result of the acquisition of 
Windom was $10,654,419. United also included the 
FSLIC cash contribution of $1,950,000 as capital. In 
financial reports filed with FSLIC and FHLBB since 
the supervisory merger, United has consistently 
booked supervisory goodwill as an asset included in 
capital for regulatory purposes, in accordance with, 
and in reliance upon, FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s express 
agreements. United has also consistently amortized 
the supervisory goodwill over a period of 35 years on 
a straight line basis, in accordance with the agree- 
ments. Prior to the enactment of FIRREA, neither 
FSLIC nor FHLBB ever objected to United’s 
treatment of supervisory goodwill. 

40. FSLIC and FHLBB benefited enormously from 
the use of supervisory goodwill in connection with the 
supervisory merger of United and Windom. At tie 
time of the merger, the fair market value of 
Windom’s liabilities far exceeded that of its assets by 
$14.3 million. Liquidating Windom at that time would 
have cost FSLIC considerably more than $143 
million, due to resolution costs such as brokerage fees 
and holding costs. Moreover, if the purchase method 


23 


of accounting for the supervisory merger had not 
been used and FSLIC and FHLBB had been required 
to provide tangible financial assistance to Winstar and 
United at pre-1981 levels, it would have cost FSLIC 
and FHLBB approximately $11 million to induce and 
arrange the supervisory merger. 

41. After the merger with Windom, United’s 
tangible net worth (i.e., exclusive of supervisory 
goodwill) was approximately negative $6.7 million. 
Obviously the purchase method of accounting for the 
proposed merger was integral to Winstar and United. 
Had Winstar and United not been permitted to 
record on United’s books supervisory goodwill in the 
amount by which Windom’s liabilities exceeded the 
fair market value of its assets, their acquisition of 


‘Windom on the financial terms proposed in their bid 


would have rendered United insolvent and subject to 
liquidation by FSLIC and FHLBB. Winstar and 
United would not have entered into such a transac- 
tion. Nor would they have made a proposal or entered 
into a transaction for the acquisition of Windom that 
did not include a lengthy amortization period in which 
to return Windom to profitability and to gradually 
replace the supervisory goodwill with tangible assets. 
Thus, Winstar’s willingness to form United and to 
acquire Windom on the financial terms proposed in 
the bid, and Winstar’s willingness to invest $2 million 
in United, were absolutely conditioned on FHLBB’s 
and FSLIC’s agreement to permit United to report 
supervisory goodwill as an asset included in capital 
for regulatory purposes and to amortize that super- 
visory goodwiil over a 35-year period. Absent the 
specified accounting treatment, Winstar and United 
either would have insisted upon receiving more than 
$5,644,500 in tangible financial assistance from FSLIC 
or would have withdrawn the offer. In order to 


24 


maintain United’s tangible capital at 3 percent of 
liabilities, the regulatory minimum prevailing at the 
time, FSLIC would have had to contribute $15.8 
million in cash or other tangible financial assistance. 

42. In 1988 Winstar purchased additional common 
stock of United for an additional $800,000. Winstar 
would not have made this investment in the absence 
of the United States’ agreement to the specified 
accounting treatment for goodwill, or if Winstar or 
United had foreseen that the government would 
renege on that agreement. 

43. Winstar and United have complied in good faith 
with all the terms and conditions of the agreements 
relating to the supervisory merger and have other- 
wise fully performed their undertakings in connection 
therewith. 

44. Since the acquisition of Windom by Winstar and 
United, the institution’s financial position has greatly 
improved. As of September 30, 1989 it would have 
cost approximately $13.2 million to liquidate United, 
more than $1 million less than the estimated $14.3 
million cost to liquidate Windom before the merger. 

45. FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s contractual promises, 
for which Winstar and United provided substantial 
consideration and on which Winstar and United 
detrimentally relied in fully performing their obliga- 
tions under their agreements with FSLIC and 
FHLBB, entitle United to record supervisory good- 
will arising from the above-described supervisory 
merger as an asset included in capital for purposes of 
regulatory capital requirements. Winstar aad 
United’s contract rights arising out of the above- 
described agreements with FSLIC and FHLBB 
constitute property within the meaning of the Fifth 
Amendment to the United States Constitution. 


25 
FIRREA 


46. FIRREA was signed into law by President 
Bush on August 9, 1989. Section 301 of FIRREA 
establishes OTS as an office in the Department of the 
Treasury. Section 301 also establishes the position of 
the Director of OTS, who shall be subject to the 
general oversight of the Secretary of the Treasury, 
except that the Secretary may not intervene in any 
matter or proceeding before the Director unless 
otherwise provided by law. The Director is the chief 
regulator of the thrift industry, empowered by 
FIRREA to conduct examinations, proscribe regula- 
tions, and issue orders as necessary to insure the safe 


and sound operation of thrifts. 


47. Section 301 of FIRREA requires the Director of 
OTS to promulgate final regulations establishing 
minimum capital requirements for federally insured 
thrifts. These minimum capital requirements became 
effective December 7, 1989, and require federally 
insured thrifts to: 


(a) maintain “tangible capital,” which excludes 
supervisory goodwill, in an amount not less than 
1.5 percent of the thrift’s assets; 

(b) maintain “core capital,” as defined in the 
statute, in an amount not less than 3 percent of 
the thrift’s assets; and 

(c) maintain “risk-based capital” in an amount not 
materially less than that required by the 
Comptroller of the Currency for national banks. 


48. FIRREA provides that “tangible capital” shall 
not include supervisory goodwill, and that only 
limited amounts of “core capital” may be made up of 
supervisory goodwill. Prior to January 1, 1992, the 
amount of supervisory goodwill includable in core 


capital may not exceed 1.5 percent of total assets. 
Thereafter, the amount of supervisory goodwill (as a 
percentage of assets) includable in core capital 
declines each year, in accordance with the following 
schedule: 


January 1, 1991, to December 


31, 1992— 1.0 percent 
January 1, 1993, to December 

31, 1993— 0.75 percent 
January 1, 1994, to December 

31, 1994— 0.375 percent 
BROSGEEE acocccccoscsenscsesusenl 0 percent 


For purposes of the minimum capital requirements, 
supervisory goodwill must be amortized over a period 
not to exceed 20 years. 

49. FIRREA also requires the Director of OTS to 
enforce the following sanctions against federally 
insured thrifts that do not meet the minimum capital 
standards promulgated under FIRREA: 


(a) After January 1, 1991, the Director must 
prohibit the asset growth of any federally 
insured thrift not in compliance with regulatory 
capital standards, except that the Director may 
approve a “limited growth exception” upon 
specified conditions. In addition, the Director 
may restrict the asset growth of any such thrift 
at any time prior to January 1, 1991. 

(b) No later than January 6, 1990, the Director 
must require thrifts that do not meet minimum 
capital standards under FIRREA to submit a 
plan that (1) addresses the thrift’s need for 
increased capital under FIRREA; (2) describes 
the manner in which the thrift will increase its 
capital to achieve the minimum capital stand- 
ards; (3) specifies types and levels of activities 


27 


the thrift will engage in; (4) requires any 
increase in assets to be accompanied by 
specified increases in tangible and risk-based 
capital; and (5) is acceptable to the Director. 
The Director must treat any material failure to 
comply with such a plan as an unsafe and 
unsound practice. 

(c) After January 1, 1991, the Director must 
require federally insured thrifts that do not 
meet minimum capital standards under FIR- 
REA to comply with a capital directive which 
may include restrictions on the payment of 
dividends and on compensation, as the Director 
determines to be appropriate. FIRREA per- 
mits, but does not require, the Director to 
exempt thrifts from receipt of a capital directive 
in certain circumstances. 

50. The Director may impose other sanctions on 

federally insured thrifts that do not meet minimum 
capital standards promulgated under FIRREA: 


(a) The Director, in his discretion, may treat any 
thrift’s failure to maintain capital at minimum 
regulatory levels as an unsafe or unsound 
practice subjecting the thrift to a range of 
further actions by the Director, including, 
among other things, issuance of cease and desist 
orders, appointment of a conservator or re- 
ceiver, and suspension or removal of officers 
and directors. 

(b) Such thrifts also must notify the Director of the 
proposed addition of any individual tu the board 
of directors or of the employment of any 
individual as a senior executive officer of the 
thrift 30 days before such addition or employ- 
ment becomes effective, and the Director may 


28 


before the end of that 30 days disapprove the 
addition or employment. 


51. Federally insured thrifts that do not meet 
minimum capital standards are characterized by 
FIRREA as “troubled institutions” and beginning 
February 4, 1990, will be prohibited form accepting 
brokered deposits subject only to a case-by-case 
waiver by the FDIC. 


OTS CAPITAL REGULATION 


52. On November 8, 1989, OTS published minimum 
capital regulations (“OTS capital regulation”) pur- 
suant to FIRREA. The OTS capital regulation 
became effective on December 7, 1989. 

53. In accordance with FIRREA, the OTS capital 
regulation establishes a minimum “tangible” capital 
requirement of 1.5 percent of total assets and a 
minimum “core” capital requirement of 3 percent of 
total assets. Supervisory goodwill cannot be counted 
as “tangible” capital at all, and can be counted as 
“core” capital only in accordance with the phase-out 
schedule set forth in FIRREA (see paragraph 48 
above). 

54. FIRREA does not require that supervisory 
goodwill be excluded from capital for purposes of 
determining compliance with minimum “risk-based” 
capital requirements. Nonetheless, the OTS capital 
regulation permits the inclusion of supervisory good- 
will in capital for purposes of the minimum “risk- 
based” capital requirement only in accordance with 
the phase-out schedule set forth in FIRREA for 
determining compliance with the minimum “core” 
capital requirement (see paragraph 48 above). 

55. Apart from the requirements and restrictions 
imposed by FIRREA on. federally insured thrift 


institutions that fail to meet minimum capital require- 
ments, the OTS capital regulation mandates a variety 
of additional consequences. For example, in addition 
to submitting a capital plan for achieving compliance 
with the minimal capital requirements, a non- 
complying thrift must, under the OTS capital regula- 
tion, certify that while the plan is under review by 
OTS, it will not (absent OTS approval) (1) grow 
beyond net interest credited, (2) make any capital 
distributions, or (3) act inconsistently with any other 
limitations on activities established by statute, regula- 
tion, or by OTS in supervisory guidance. If a plan is 
not approved by OTS, the submitting thrift “shall 
immediately and without any further action” refrain 
from increasing its assets beyond the amount held on 
the day it receives notice of the disapproval and 
comply with any other restrictions or limitations set 
forth in OTS’s written disapproval of the plan. The 
OTS capital regulation further provides that OTS 
may, “through enforcement proceedings or other- 
wise,” require any thrift that fails to meet any of its 
regulatory capital requirements to take one or more 
of the following corrective actions: 


(a) increase the amount of its regulatory capital to 
a specified level or levels’ 

(b) convene a meeting or meetings with the OTS 
supervision staff for the purpose of accomplish- 
ing the objectives of this section; 

(c) reduce the rate of earnings that may be paid on 
savings accounts; | 
(d) limit the receipt of deposits to those made to 

existing accounts; 

(e) cease or limit the issuance of new accounts of 
any or all classes or categories, except in 
exchange for existing accounts; 


30 


(f) cease or limit lending or the making of a 
particular type or category of loan; 

(g) cease or limit the purchase of loans or the 
making of specified other investments; 

(h) limit operational expenditures to specified 
levels; 

(i) increase liquid assets and maintain such in- 
creased liquidity at specified levels; or 

(j) take such other action or actions as the Director 
may deem necess y or appropriate for the 
safety and soundness of the savings association, 
or depositors or investors in the savings 
association. 


On or after January 1, 1991, these restrictions, “and 
any other restrictions the Director [of OTS] deter- 
mines appropriate,” may be included in a “capital 
directive” issued by the Director against a non- 
complying thrift. Finally, according to the OTS capital 
regulation, OTS must treat as an unsafe and unsound 
practice “any material failure by a savings association 
to comply with any plan, regulation, written agree- 
ment undertaken or order or directive issued to 
comply with the requirements of [the capital 
regulation].” 

56. In “Thrift Bulletin 36,” now effective, OTS 
specified that acceptance of a capital plan submitted 
by a non-complying thrift will be predicated on 
certain additional stringent conditions, including 
among others: 


(a) The management and the board of directors 
must execute an operating agreement which 
may provide, among other things, that if 
specific targets within the plan are not met or 
the thrift takes any action that does not 
comport with the plan, certain activities will be 


31 


significantly restricted, a consent to merge 
agreement will be executed, or management 
and the board of directors will resign. 

(b) The board of directors and senior management 
for the thrift must file an amendment, accept- 
able to the OTS District Director, to the capital 
plan within 15 days following an event or 
circumstance that materially affects the validity 
of the capital plan or its underlying assump- 
tions. At a minimum, the capital plan must be 
reviewed and revised as necessary on an annual 
basis. 

(c) The thrift must submit to the OTS District 
Director, within 20 days following the close of 
each calendar quarter, quarterly variance re- 
ports comparing actual capital to the targets 
established in the capital plan. Failure to file 
such variance reports shall constitute an unsafe 
and unsound practice. Variances that the Dis- 
trict Director deems to be material may be 
grounds for revoking acceptance of the capital 
plan. 


57. According to Thrift Bulletin 36, a thrift that 
fails to comply with any of its minimum capital 
requirements will be deemed to be in non-compliance 
upon “adoption” of the OTS capital regulation, rather 
than upon the rejection of any capital plan submitted 
by the thrift. 

58. Thrift Bulletin 36 recognizes that, apart from 
the specific restrictions set forth in FIRREA and the 
OTS capital regulation, there are a significant number 
of related OTS regulations, rules, and policy state- 
ments that limit or restrict a variety of essential 
business activities (e.g., payment of dividends, 
establishment of branches, receipt of Federal Home 
Loan Bank advances, investments in securities and 


32 


real estate), by thrifts that do not meet minimum 
capital requirements. Thrift Bulletin 36 requires that 
thrifts “request and receive approval of specific, 
express exemptions” from the operation of such 
related regulations “as part of the capital plan to be 
deemed in capital compliance.” 

59. Because of the provisions of FIRREA and the 
OTS capital regulation excluding supervisory goodwill 
from capital for regulatory reporting purposes, 
United cannot currently satisfy the minimum capital 
requirements established by FIRREA and the OTS 
regulation and cannot come into compliance with such 
requirements in the foreseeable future. 


COUNT I 


60. Plaintiffs incorporate by reference paragraphs 1 
through 59 above. 

61. Winstar invested $2 million to acquire Windom 
in express reliance on the United States’ agreement 
that, for regulatory purposes, the supervisory good- 
will resulting from the acquisition of Windom was to 


be treated as an asset includable in the capital of 


United and amortized on a straight-line basis over a 
period not to exceed 35 years. Winstar later invested 
$800,000 in United in reliance on the same agreement. 
Also in reliance on the agreement, Winstar and 
United have operated and managed United since the 
merger, improving its financial position and greatly 
reducing the cost of liquidating United. 

62. The United States has breached its contract 
with Winstar and United by refusing to allow 
United’s supervisory goodwill to be treated as an 
asset included in the capital of that institution, and by 
refusing to allow that the supervisory goodwill be 
amortized over 35 years, for regulatory purposes. 


33 


63. Winstar and United are entitled to restitution in 
the amount of Winstar’s investment of $2.8 million, 
plus the return of all benefits conferred upon the 
government through plaintiffs’ purchase, operation, 
and management of Windom. Winstar and United 
currently estimate the value of such benefits, which is 
to be determined at trial, to exceed $1 million. 


COUNT II 


64. Plaintiffs incorporate by reference paragraphs 1 
through 63 above. 

65. The parties’ agreement concerning the treat- 
ment of goodwill was an essential term and condition 
of the transaction, and was essential to the parties’ 
purpose of allowing Winstar and United a reasonable 
opportunity to turn Windom from a failing institution 
into a profitable venture. 

66. Winstar and United would not have agreed to 
acquire Windom in the absence of such accounting 
treatment, because United would have been left 
insolvent and because United would otherwise have 
been unable to manage itself without substantial 
regulatory oversight that would interfere with its 
business and prospects. 

‘ . The parties to the transaction could not 
reasonably have foreseen that Congress would enact 
legislation that would be construed to extinguish 
Winstar and United’s existing contract rights. 

68. FIRREA’s abrogation of the United States’ 
contractual agreements renders United hopelessly 
insolvent, frustrates the entire purpose of Winstar 
and United’s contract with the government for the 
acquisition of Windom, and deprives Winstar and 
United of the consideration for which they bargained. 


34 


69. Winstar and United are entitled to rescission of 
the transaction with the United States, and the 
return of Winstar’s investment of $2.8 million, plus 
the return of all benefits conferred upon the 
government through plaintiffs’ purchase, operation, 
and management of Windom. Winstar and United 
currently estimate the value of such benefits, which is 
to be determined at trial, to exceed $1 million. 


COUNT III 


70. Plaintiffs incorporate by reference paragraphs 1 
through 69 above.

capital requirements constitute a repudiation and 
abrogation of Winstar and United’s contract rights, as 
described above, and effect a taking of Winstar and 
United’s property without just compensation, in 
violation of the Fifth Amendment to the United 
States Constitution. 

72. Winstar and United are entitled to just 
compensation in the amount of their investment of 
$2.8 million, plus the return of all benefits conferred 
upon the government through plaintiffs’ purchase, 
operation, and management of Windom. Winstar and 
United currently estimate the value of such benefits,

million.

RE: Accounting Treatment For the Merger of Win- 
dom Federal Savings and Loan Association into 
United Federal Savings Bank (an entity to be 
formed) 


Dear Mr. Halsne: 


As you are aware on May 3 and 4, 1984, Mr. E. Ted 
Yoch and Mr. Gregg Schneider, both of Rosewood 
Corporation, Mr. James Stephenson of the Faegre & 
Benson law firm in Minneapolis and myself met in 
Washington D.C. with members of the Federal 
Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation and the 
Office of the General Counsel. 


During the meeting we discussed the fact that 
perhaps a better approach to the accounting treat- 
ment for the assistance from FSLIC could be utilized. 
It was felt that rather than recording the entire 
amount of the assistance as a permanent increase in 
the net worth of United Federal Savings Bank it 
might be better to split the assistance between net 
worth and a reduction in the recorded goodwill. 


Accordingly, United Federal Savings Bank requests 
approval through this letter for recording the finan- 
cial assistance received from the Federal Savings and 


41 


Loan Insurance Corporation at the date of acquisition 
in the following manner: 


1. The amount of assistance applicable to specific 
scheduled loans in an amount totaling $1,950,000 to 
be recorded as a permanent increase in the net 
worth of United Federal Savings Bank. 


2. The balance of the assistance which will be an 
amount equal to the negative net worth of Windom 
Federal Savings and Loan Association at the date 
of closing to be recorded as a reduction of the 
goodwill recorded in connection with the formation 
of United Federal Savings Bank. 


If the accounting treatment requested herein is 


permitted, United Federal Savings Bank will have 


beginning net worth of $3,950,000 resulting from the 
$1,950,000 assistance from FSLIC and $2,000,000 
injected by Winstar Corporation. This will mean that 
United Federal Savings Bank will begin its opera- 
tions with an approximately 6.8% capital to asset 
ratio. It will also mean that the recorded goodwill at 
the date of acquisition will be reduced by the amount 
applicable to negative net worth which we estimate to 
be approximately $3,100,000, assuming that closing 
occurs prior to June 1, 1984. This will benefit United 
Federal Savings Bank’s future operations since the 
amortization of the goodwill over the 35 year period 
will be reduced. 


This request will modify the accounting treatment 
for the FSLIC assistance asked for in our letter 
dated December 6, 1983 to the attention of Ms. 


- Margaret M. Meagher, which was signed by Duane D. 


Taut, Partner. All other matters in the December 6, 
1983 letter will remain as stated. During our 


42 


discussion in Washington it was felt that this 
modification would hopefully not delay or present an 
impediment to the closing of this transaction at your 
earliest convenience. If you have any questions 


regarding this request, please do not hesitate to 
contact me. 


Very truly yours, 


Robert E. Hiller 
Partner 


Encl.

MEMORANDUM

TO: JJ. Finn 
Secretary to the Board 


FROM: Harlan G. Halsne 
Supervisory Agent 


SUBJECT: S-Memorandum—Proposed Supervisory 
Conversion of: 


Windom FS&LA 
Windom, Minnesota 
Docket No. 3058 


from the Federal Mutual to Federal Stock Savings 
Bank Form of Organization 


The Merger of Windom FS&LA 
with 


United Federal Savings Bank, a de novo stock saving 
bank wholly-owned by: 


Winstar Corporation, a newly formed holding 
company 


1. Proposal 
Winstar Corporation (Winstar), a newly formed 


Minnesota Corporation, proposes to acquire Win- 
dom Federal Savings and Loan Association, 


51 


Windom, Minnesota (Windom). The transaction will 
involve the supervisory conversion of Windom 
Federal Savings and Loan Association from a 
federally-chartered mutual savings and loan asso- 
ciation to a federally-chartered stock savings bank. 
Following the conversion, Windom Federal Savings 
and Loan Association will be merged with United 
Federal Savings Bank (United FSB), a de novo 
wholly-owned subsidiary of Winstar. 


The Office of Examinations and Supervision (OES) 
has no supervisory objection to this proposal. The 
remainder of this memorandum will discuss its 
financial and supervisory aspects. 


_ IL. Condition of Failing Association—Windom 


FS&LA 


As of April 30, 1984, Windom’s assets totalled 
$49.2 million, with a-.deficit net worth of $3.1 
million. Insolvency resulted from both substan- 
tial operating losses and substantial actual or 
indicated losses on loans and real estate owned. 
Operating losses were incurred during the past 
several years due to (1) increased cost of 
savings resulting from a shift in savings capital 
to higher rate accounts; (2) excessive levels of 
borrowed money resulting from the need to 
fund nationwide loan participation commit- 
ments; and (3) falling yields resulting from high 
levels of non- or low-earning assets. Actual or 
indicated losses on loans and real estate owned 
were incurred or recognized over the past 
several rears primarily as a result of deficient 
underwriting of major nationwide participation 
loans. The following table summarizes Windom’s 
relevant financial history:

Windom’s existing problem assets are briefly 
summarized as follows: 


A. Perkins Road Development, Inc.—122 Unit 


Ramada Inn—Stillwater, Oklahoma—Loan to 
Facilitate Sale of Real Estate Owned 


Perkins Road Development, Inc. (Perkins) was 
formed by Windom and three other origina! loan 
participants to dispose of a 122 unit motel 
acquired by deed in lieu of foreclosure. Perkins 
sold the property in 1982. The sale involved 
below market rate financing, deferred equity 
contributions and other favorable terms. As of 
December 31, 1983, Perkins’ investments total- 
led $3,908,959. Windom’s share equals 
$1,200,113. There is no current appraisal of this 
property. As of June 1, 1984, payments were 
$245,000 in arrears. The buyers have invested 
$510,000 equity in the project. 


. Carson, Inc.—Macienda Taos—13 Unit Con- 


dominium Project—Taos, New Mexico—Real 
Estate Owned 


Carson, Inc. was formed by Windom and six 
other original loan participants to complete and 
market a 13 unit condominium acquired by deed 
in lieu of foreclosure. Total investment in this 
project as of July 31, 1983 totalled $4,681,676. A 
May, 1983 appraisal estimated value at 
$1,750,000 indicating a $2,931,575 loss. Windom’s 
share of this loss equals $1,064,187. A specific 
reserve was established in this amount in June, 
1983. 


od 


C. Tonapah, Nevada—30 Townhouses—Real 
Estate Owned 


This property was acquired by deed in lieu of 
foreclosure in October, 1982. The association’s 
75 percent interest totalled $1,217,378 as of 
July 31, 1983. The most recent appraisal 
indicated a loss of $159,878 for Windom. A 
specific reserve was established in this amount 
in September, 1983. 


D. Other Major Scheduled Loans 


Windom’s interest in five (5) other major 
scheduled loans and real estate owned totalled 
$1,926,781 as of July 31, 1983. Appraisals 
indicated losses totalling $74,880 on two of the 
projects. Specific reserves have been 
established in the amount of $66,200. 


III. Condition of Industry in Des Moines Bank 


IV. 


District 
There are 199 insured institutions in the Eighth 


FHLBB District. Assets of these insured institu- 
tions totalled $43.6 billion as of March 31, 1984. 


As of March 31, 1984, OES projects that eight (8) 
Eighth District insured institutions, representing 
assets of .8 billion dollars of 1.8 percent of total 
Eighth District insured assets, will be insolvent 
within the next twelve months. 


Supervisory History 


Primary supervisory concerns in Windom’s most 
recent regular and special examination reports 


55 


were directed to (a) poor operating results and 
net worth position and (b) serious nationwide 
participation loan underwriting deficiencies which 
resulted in indicated losses and high levels of low- 
and non-earning assets. The need for close moni- 
toring became apparent in December, 1981 when 
a regular examination first disclosed large in- 
creases in scheduled items and numerous par- 
ticipation loan underwriting deficiencies. A subse- 
quent follow-up examination conducted in July, 
1982 disclosed the possibility of significant indi- 
cated losses and a rapidly declining net worth 
position. Upon receipt of this follow-up report, 
supervision immediately arranged a meeting with 
Windom’s Board. The discussion at the meeting 
focused on the possibility of Windom’s continued 
existence in light of operating results and possible 
indicated losses. The association did not agree 
that the indicated losses were warranted. They 
were given until December, 1982 to support their 
contention. The Board was apprised of what ac- 
tions would be necessary should the association’s 
position continue to deteriorate. It was suggested 
that the Board begin to consider all options. 


The next development took place in January, 1983 
when a review of the December, 1982 monthly 
report disclosed significantly greater operating 
losses than anticipated and a consequent potential 
for insolvency by March, 1983. When contacted, 
management indicated that the December results 
were due to extraordinary adjustments and that 
1983 losses would be manageable. In early 1983, 
Supervision assisted Windom’s attempt to obtain 
net worth certificates. However, during March, 
1983, it was determined that insolvency could not 
be avoided even with net worth certificates. 


56 


Operations had continued to decline, and the 
receipt of appraisals confirmed the existence of 
indicated losses on association real estate owned 
and participation loans. The association became 
insolvent in March, 1983 upon the establishment 
of certain requested specific reserves. Windom’s 
Board signed a Consent Resolution on March 11, 
1983. The resolution provided, among other 
things, that the FSLIC and Supervision could 
negotiate for Windom, and that the Windom 
Board would approve any resulting plan of 
merger, consolidation, or transfer of assets and 
liabilities. Supervision worked towards an unas- 
sisted merger until additional indicated losses on 
association assets made such a_ solution 
impossible. 


The following table provides information regard- 
ing Windom’s recent examination report ratings: 


Composite CRA 


Date Exam Type Evaluation Evaluation 


8-15-83 Special Limited 40 3 
2-24-83 Supervisory 40 3 
7-29-82 Special Limited NA NA 
12-07-81 Supervisory 20 3 


Since the Special Limited Examination dated 


August 16, 1983, Windom has continued to incur 
substantial operating losses primarily as a result of 
high levels of low- and non-earning assets and the 
continued shift in savings capital to higher rate 
accounts. 


V. Pledged Accounts 


There are no pledged accounts at Windom. 


57 


VI. Condition of Acquirer—Winstar Corporation, 


Roseville, Minnesota 


A. General 

Winstar Cerporation is a newly formed cor- 
poration, which, if its application for acquisi- 
tion of Windom is approved, will become a 
holding company. Winstar will be capitalized 
with $2,250,000 in cash, of which $1,750,000 
will be borrowed from Norwest Bank, Min- 
neapolis. The balance of $500,000 will be 
contributed by the five principals. The 
$1,750,000 to be borrowed from Norwest will 
be secured by 100 percent of the capital stock 
of United FSB issued at the time of its 
organization. Winstar principals will person- 
ally guarantee $500,000 of this debt. Interest 
will be at prime plus 1 percent, adjustable 
annually. Repayment will be according to a 
“Minimum Principal Debt Reduction Sched- 
ule”. (See Exhibit S-14). Norwest reserves the 
right to alter the terms and conditions of the 
credit at each annual renewal. 


Winstar will initially capitalize United FSB by 
purchasing its stock for $2,000,000 cash. 


Winstar will pay a $100,000 brokerage fee to 
Newlin Bank Sales in connection with the 
acquisition of Windom. The fee, at 5 percent of 
sales price, involves an assumption by Winstar 
of a finder’s fee obligation of Douglas Kratz. 
Kratz’s obligation arose out of a written 
agreement between Newlin Bank Sales and 
himself to pay a 5 percent sales price finder’s 
fee upon the consumation of any purchase of 
any institutions brokered by Newlin Bank 


58 


Sales. Newlin Bank Sales had originally presented 
the Windom opportunity to Kratz. Kratz would 
have been obligated to pay the fee upon consuma- 
tion of purchase. Kratz, however, chose not to 
pursue this investment. Kratz presented the 
Windom opportunity to the organizers of Winstar 
on the condition that if the transaction was 
consumated, Winstar would pay the $100,000 
Newlin fee, plus a $15,000 fee to Kratz. These fees 
will be paid entirely from Winstar funds. No fees 
will be paid from FSLIC funds infused into the 
institution as FSLIC assistance. We have no 
objection to the payment of these fees. 


The five (5) principals of Winstar have a combined 
net worth exceeding 14 million dollars. This 
amount consists primarily of real estate equities 
and stock in closely-held corporations. The corpo- 
rations are involved in mortgage banking ac- 
tivities and in real estate management, finance, 
development, and syndication. Net worth of the 
principals are thus concentrated in non-liquid real 
estate and real estate related investments. Net 
income of the principals ranged from $82,000 to 
$131,750 and aggregated $505,000 in 1983. 


One of the principals of Winstar, E. Ted Yoch, 
was director of Northern Federal Savings and 
Loan Association, Minneapolis, Minnesota, from 
1978 to 1981. Northern was the subject of a 
supervisory merger in 1983. Mr. Yoch’s selection 
as a director was pursuant to a supervisory 
request that Northern replace certain board 
members with outsiders. The selection of Mr. 
Yoch was approved by Supervision. We have no 
objection to Mr. Yoch as a principal in the 
acquisition of Windom. 


59 


B. Business Plan 
Winstar has prepared a comprehensive busi- 
ness plan for United FSB. (See Exhibit S-13). 
The plan meets the content requirements of 
SP-24, and appears to present a reasonable 
formuia for the initial operation of United 
FSB. We have no supervisory objection to the 
business plan. In addition to reviewing the 
business plan, we have reviewed the viability 
analysis prepared by the Analysis and Evalua- 
tions Division FSLIC. In our opinion, the 
association will be viable following conversion. 


C. Holding Company Act Standards 


We find no statutory basis for disapproval of 
the application of Winstar for approval to 
become a savings and loan holding company 
through its acquisition and control of Windom. 
Pursuant to Section 584.4(c) of the Holding 
Company Regulations, this office, in conjunc- 
tion with the Office of the FLIC, has 
reviewed the financial and managerial re- 
sources and future prospects of Winstar. We 
have no reason to believe that the proposed 
acquisition of Windom would be deterimental 
to the savings accountholders of Windom or 
the insurance risk of FSLIC. 


D. Change of Control Issues 


Winstar is a de novo holding company. There 
are no change of control issues or require- 
ments in connection with this proposal. 


E. Community Revinvestment Act 


Windom’s recent examination reports indicate 
satisfactory CRA ratings. The business plan 
prepared by Winstar for United FSB appears 


60 


to establish community reinvestment goals and 
policies which would, if adhered to, provide 
continued CRA compliance. 


VII. Personnel Issues 


Winstar proposes to retain an advisory board at 
the Windom United FSB office to consist of 
John Gelle, Ted M. Weeks, August W. Lerner, 
Roger L. Benson, J.A. Dick and Frank C. 
Jungas, all current Windom Board members. 
The advisory board will meet monthly. Each 
member will receive $100 per meeting attended. 
The advisory board’s responsibilities will be to 
generate goodwill and new business, participate 
in planning meetings, and give advice on 
borrower credit worthiness, marketing strategy 
and history of troubled loans. Each member will 
serve at the pleasure of the Board of United 
FSB. 


The principal managment change will be the 
addition of Thomas C. Daniel as Chief Executive 
Officer of United FSB. Winstar entered into an 
employment contract with Mr. Daniel on June 1, 
1984. (See Exhibit S-9(e). We have reviewed this 
contract and find that it conforms to the 
requirements of Sections 563.39 and 571.5(d)(4) 
of the Insurance Regulations, and Section 
545.122 of the Federal Regulations. Mr. Daniel 
apparently has 12 years experience in various 
phases of finance and banking. We have no 
objection to the selection of Mr. Daniel as Chief 
Executive Officer of the proposed United FSB. 


FSLIC has agreed to allow United FSB to be 
governed by a Board of Directors of between 
6 and 15 members. This is below the 


61 


standard seven (7) member minimum charter 
requirement of Section 552.3. We have no 
objection. 


VIII. Stipulations 


A. Requested Dividend Restriction Waiver 


Winstar has requested that United FSB be 
allowed to pay dividends from available net 
income as follows: 


1984 50% 
1985 50% 
1986 60% 
1987 70% 
1988 60% 


1989 and after 50% 


The Analysis and Evaluations Division of FSLIC 
has estimated that with the requested dividend 
restriction waiver, the dividends from United 
FSB wil permit Winstar to meet its debt service 
requirements. FSLIC has agreed to the re- 
quested dividend forbearance. We have no objec- 
tion to this request. We recommend that the 
Resolution limit payment of dividends to 50 
percent of available net income, but that the 
limitation be waived during the years and for the 
percentages requested by Winstar. The for- 
bearance letter attached at Exhibit S-12, 
provides the waiver of the dividend restriction 
for the years requested by Winstar. 


B. Net Worth Maintenance Restriction 


We recommend that Winstar be required to 
maintain net worth at the level required by 
Section 563.13(b) of the Insurance Regulations 


62 


for institutions insured 20 years or more 
without regard to any net worth assistance 
for which United FSB may qualify, and to 
infuse additional equity if necessary. We also 
recommend that United FSB be required to 
waive any right to net worth assistance 
under the net worth certificate program. 


C. Stock Issuance and Transfer Restriction 


We recommend that Winstar and United 
FSB be required to stipulate to certain 
stock issuance and transfer restrictions. 
Specifically, that, for the term of the 
Assistance Agreement or three years from 
the date of the merger, whichever is longer: 


1. Winstar stipulates to maintain control 
over United FSB and to not sell, pledge 
(except as security for initial acquisitions 
to Norwest Bank, Minneapolis, Min- 
nesota), transfer, or otherwise dispose of 
United FSB stock, unless the Corpora- 
tion gives its written consent. 


2. United FSB stipulates that it will not 
issue stock, stock options or warrants to 
any person other than Winstar without 
the Corporation’s written consent. 


XI. 


63 


In a letter dated June 1, 1984 to Regional 
Director Arendes, Deputy Director Moore stated 


that purchase accounting was appropriate for the 
transaction. 


Service Corporation Issues 


There are no service corporation issues. The 
association wholly-owns Azure, Inc., and partially 
owns two other service corporations. The ac- 
tivities of these three corporations are limited to 
the salvage of Windom problem assets. 


Forbearances 


In addition to their request for a waiver of the 
dividend restrictions for 1986, 1987 and 1988 as 
discussed above, United FSB has requested 
three standard forbearances presently approved 
by the Bank Board in connection with superv- 
visory mergers. A copy of the forbearance 
language is attached at Exhibit S-12. We have no 


objection to the granting of these additional 
forbearances. 


/s/ Harlan G. Halsne

FEDERAL HOME LOAN BANK BOARD 


Acquisition of Windom Federal Savings 
and Loan Association 
No. 84-363 
Date: July 6, 1984 


Recitals 


WHEREAS, the board of directors of Windom 
Federal Savings and Loan Association, Windom, 
Minnesota (“Windom”), has adopted, by at least a 
two-thirds vote of the board, a plan of merger 
evidenced by a Combination Agreement and Plan of 
Merger (“Merger Agreement”) (a copy of which is in 
the Minute Exhibit File), pursuant to which Windom 
will merge with and into United Federal Savings 
Bank, (“Savings Bank”), a proposed federal stock 
savings bank; and 


WHEREAS, Winstar Corporation, a Minnesota cor- 
poration (“Winstar” or “Applicant”), has applied to 
the Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation 
(“FSLIC”), pursuant to § 408 of the National Housing 
Act, as amended, (the “Act”), 12 U.S.C. § 1730 (1982), 
for prior written approval to acquire control of 
Windom through the merger of Windom with and into 
Savings Bank, a proposed federal stock savings bank 
to be organized pursuant to § 5(p) of the Home 
Owners’ Loan Act of 1933, as amended, (“HOLA”), 12 
U.S.C. § 1464(p) (1982), the stock of which is to be 
purchased and held by Winstar, and the Federal 
Home Loan Bank Board (“Bank Board”), as operating 
head of the FSLIC, has considered the Holding 
Company Application and accompanying materials of 
Applicant (hereafter referred to as the “Application”); 
and 


66 


WHEREAS, Applicant, as the organizer of Savings 
Bank, has applied to the Bank Board for approval of 
the merger of Windom into Savings Bank (“Merger”); 
and 


WHEREAS, The acquisition by Applicant of control 
of Windom and its successor Savings Bank (“Acquisi- 
tion”) and the Merger are conditioned upon the 
execution of an Assistance Agreement by and among 
Winstar, Savings Bank, and the FSLIC, under which 
the FSLIC will provide certain financial assistance 
and indemnification to Savings Bank to facilitate the 
Merger; and 


WHEREAS, The Bank Board has considered pro- 
posed forms of the following documents: Plan of 
Organization, Application for Insurance of Accounts, 
Agreement for Operating Policies, Federal Stock 
Charter, Bylaws, Stockholders’ Consent to Action, 
Directors’ Consent to Action, Directors’ Resolutions, 
Stock Certificate, Combination Agreement and Plan 
of Merger, Assistance Agreement, Net Worth Mainte- 
nance Stipulation, Stock Transfer Stipulation, Stock 
Issuance Stipulation, Dividend Limitation Stipulation, 
Stipulation of Affiliate Status, and Stipulation 
restricting Securities Brokerage Activities; and 


WHEREAS, The Bank Board has considered memo- 
randa from its Office of Examinations and Supervi- 
sion (“S-Memo”), dated June 28, 1984, Office of the 
Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation 
(“A-Memo”), dated June 29, 1984, and Office of 
General Counsel (“L-Memo”), dated July 3, 1984, 
together with accompanying attachments and exhibits 
(copies of which memoranda are in the Minute 
Exhibit File), with respect to Windom:

Review of Record and Proceedings 


NOW, THEREFORE, IT IS RESOLVED That the 
Bank Board has reviewed, and considered the record 
before it regarding the financial condition of Windom 
and the proposed Acquisition transaction; and 


Exercise of Authority under § 5(p) of the HOLA 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That the Bank Board 
deems and finds that: 


(1) Pursuant to approvals given in this resolution 
for the provision of assistance to facilitate the 
merger of Windom with Savings Bank, for the 
purposes of § 5(p) of the HOLA, the FSLIC has 
contracted to provide assistance to Windom 
under § 406(f) of the Act; and 

(2) Severe financial conditions exist which threaten 
the stability of Windom, and authorization of 
the chartering of Savings Bank and the Merger 
of Windom into Savings Bank is likely to 
improve the financial condition of Windom; and 

(3) The Bank Board may authorize or require the 
chartering of Savings Bank and the Merger 
pursuant to § 5(p) of the HOLA; and

Supervisory Conversion 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That the Bank Board, as 
operating head of the FSLIC, finds that: 


(1) Windom is insolvent in that its assets are less 
than its obligations to creditors and others, 
including its members; 

(2) The Bank Board has the power to appoint a 
receiver for the purpose of liquidating Windom; 

(3) Upon the liquidation of Windom, accompanied 
by the payment of insurance on accounts, the 
members of Windom would have no realizable 
equity interest in Windom; 

(4) Pursuant to this Resolution, the FSLIC has 
contracted to provide assistance to Windom 
under section 406 of the Act; 

(5) Severe financial conditions exist which threaten 
the financial condition of Windom, and authori- 
zation of the supervisory merger conversion of 
Windom is likely to improve its financial 
condition; 

(6) Following the merger conversion of Windom, 
the resulting association will be a viable entity; 

(7) The conversion to stock form as part of the 
supervisory merger of Windom into Savings 
Bank is part of a supervisory transaction, and 


74 


may be and is hereby approved under § 5(i) of 
the HOLA, 12 U.S.C. § 1464(i) (1982), § 5(p) of 
the HOLA, 12 U.S.C. § 1464(p) (1982), and 
§ 402(j) of the Act, 12 U.S.C. 1725(j) (1982); and 

(8) Pursuant to the exception provisions in 
§ 563b.1(a) and § 563b.20 of the Rules and

Insurance Regulations shall not- govern the 
conversion of Windom; and

by the Applicant and contained in a proposed letter (a 
copy of which is in the Minute Exhibit File); and

Assistant Secretary is authorized and hereby directed 
to send Savings Bank a letter in the form considered 
by the Bank Board; and 


Effective Date of Merger 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That the effective date of 
the Merger of Windom with Savings Bank shall be

date and time as the Supervisory Agent shall

FEDERAL HOME LOAN BANK BOARD 


Delegation of Authority 
No. 84-364 
Date: July 6, 1984 


WHEREAS, The Director, Deputy Director, or an 
Associate Director, Office of the Federal Savings and 
Loan Insurance Corporation (“FSLIC”), or such 
persons as any of them may designate have been 
authorized by Resolution No. 84-363, dated July 6, 
1984, to execute on behalf of the FSLIC in its 
corporate capacity an Assistance Agreement with 
Winstar Corporation, Roseville, Minnesota and 
United Federal Savings Bank, Windom, Minnesota; 
and 


WHEREAS, The Assistance Agreement imposes 
certain rights, duties, and obligations upon the 
FSLIC in its corporate capacity that do not require 
basic determinations of policy; and 


WHEREAS, In the administration of such agree- 
ments, and of any rights or obligations of the FSLIC 
under them, it may be necessary or desirable that the 
FSLIC in its corporate capacity contract for legal, 
auditing, or other services: 


NOW THEREFORE, IT IS RESOLVED, That the 
Federal Home Loan Bank Board (“Bank Board”), as 
operating head of the FSLIC, determines that such 
expenditures of the FSLIC in its corporate capacity 
pursuant to the Assistance Agreement are necessary, 
made under the National Housing Act, and shall be 
incurred, allowed, and paid under the provisions of 
the fourth sentence of § 402(c)(5) of the National 


86 


Housing Act, as amended, 12 U.S.C. § 1725(c)(5) 
(1982), without regard to the provisions of any other 
law governing the expenditure of public funds; and 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That effective upon the 
execution of the Assistance Agreement, the Director 
or Deputy Director, Office of the FSLIC, or such 
person as either of them may designate is hereby 
authorized and directed to take such actions as may 
be desirable or necessary to fulfill the rights, duties, 
and obligations of the FSLIC in its corporate capacity 
under such Agreement and to incur, allow, and pay all 
expenditures in fulfillment of such rights, duties, and 
obligations, pursuant to the fourth sentence of 
§ 402(c)(5) of the National Housing Act, as amended, 
12 U.S.C. § 1725(c)(5) (1982), including, but not 
limited to: 


(1) The payment of amounts required by such 
Agreement; 

(2) Contracting for legal, accounting or other 
services; 

(3) The assignment and pursuit of claims; and 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That the General Counsel 
may also exercise the authority conferred pursuant to 
the preceding paragraph with respect to contracting 
on behalf of the FSLIC for legal or other related 
services needed to implement such agreements. 


RESOLVED FURTHER, That the Director or 
Deputy Director, Office of the FSLIC, or a person 
designated by either is authorized and directed to 
take all other actions necessary and appropriate to 
exercise the rights, and fulfill the duties and 
obligations of the FSLIC in the administration of the 
agreements. 


87 


By the Federal Home Loan 
Bank Board 


J. J. Finn
