COMPLAINT 


For their Complaint against the United States, 
plaintiffs Statesman Savings Holding Corp. (“SSHC”), 
The Statesman Group, Inc. (“Statesman Group”), and 
American Life and Casualty Insurance Company 
(“American Life”), state as follows: 


NATURE OF ACTION 


1. This action arises out of contractual promises 
made to plaintiffs by the Federal Savings and Loan 
Insurance Corporation (“FSLIC”) and the Federal 
Home Loan Bank Board (“FHLBB”) pursuant to 
their statutory supervisory authority over federally 
insured savings and loan institutions and savings 
banks (hereinafter referred to as “thrifts” or “thrift 
institutions”). As more fully described below, these 
contractual promises were made for the purpose of 
inducing plaintiffs to acquire and operate four failed 
thrift institutions. Plaintiffs’ acquisition of these 
insolvent thrift institutions obviated their liquidation 
by FSLIC and FHLBB. If FSLIC and FHLBB had 
been forced to liquidate the institutions, the present 
value cost would have exceeded $173 million. Plain- 
tiffs’ acquisitions of these institutions saved FSLIC 
and FHLBB, by their own admission, $50 million in 
liquidation costs. Plaintiffs have fully performed their 
obligations under their contract with FSLIC and 
FHLBB. Although FSLIC and FHLBB have received 
the benefits of their contractual bargain with plain- 
tiffs, they have broken their contractual promises to 
plaintiffs, partially as a result of the recently enacted 
Financial Institutions Reform, Recovery, and Enfor- 
cement Act of 1989 (“FIRREA”), Pub. Law, No. 


134 


101-75, 103 Stat. 183, and of certain regulations 
promulgated by the Office of Thrift Supervision 
(“OTS”) pursuant to FIRREA. This repudiation and 
abrogation of defendants’ contractual promises to 
plaintiffs constitute a material breach of contract that 
defeats the central object of this agreement, a taking 
of plaintiffs’ property without just compensation in 
violation of the Fifth Amendment to the United 
States Constitution, and a deprivation of plaintiffs’ 
property without due process of law in violation of 
the Fifth Amendment to the United States Constitu- 
tion. In this action, plaintiffs seek (1) a declaration 
that defendants’ actions effect a repudiation and 
material breach of their contractual obligations to 
plaintiffs and, that, thereby, effect a taking of 
plaintiffs’ property without just compensation and a 
deprivation of plaintiffs’ property without due process 
of law, in violation of the Fifth Amendment, and (2) 
an order rescinding the transaction and awarding 
plaintiffs compensation for all monies expended or 
costs incurred by them, and for the value of all 
benefits conferred on defendants, as a consequence of 
plaintiffs’ purchase, operation, and management of the 
four thrifts. 


THE PARTIES TO THE TRANSACTIONS 


2. Statesman Bank for Savings, FSB (“Statesman 
Bank”) is a conservatively managed, financially sound 
savings bank serving the needs of communities in 
lowa and Florida. Headquartered in Des Moines, 
lowa, the Bank is charted by the federal government 
and is a member of the Federal Home Loan Bank 
System. Until August 9, 1989—the date on which 
FSLIC was abolished under FIRREA—accounts of 


135 


the Bank’s depositors were insured by FSLIC. Since 
August 9, 1989, such accounts have been and are now 
insured by defendant FDIC. 

3. SSHC is a holding company organized under the 
laws of lowa. SSHC has 84,000 shares of common 
stock outstanding, all of which is owned by plaintiff 
American Life. SSHC has 126,000 shares of preferred 
stock outstanding, all of which is owned by plaintiff 
Statesman Group. SSHC owns one hundred percent 
(100%) of the issued and outstanding common stock of 
Statesman Bank, is entitled to receive all dividends 
declared by Statesman Bank, and has the right to 
receive the net assets of Statesman Bank upon the 
liquidation, dissolution or winding up of the affairs of 
Statesman Bank. 

4. Statesman Group is a publicly owned corporation 
organized under the laws of Delaware. Statesman 
Group owns all of the outstanding preferred stock of 
SSHC, and all of the outstanding common and 
preferred stock of American Life. 

5. American Life is a corporation organized under 
the laws of lowa. All of the common and preferred 
stock of American Life is owned by Statesman Group. 
American Life owns all of the outstanding common 
stock of SSHC. 

6. Prior to enactment of FIRREA, FHLBB was an 
agency of the United States. Under the Home 
Owners’ Loan Act of 1933, as amended, FHLBB was 
charged with, among other things, regulating and 
supervising federally chartered savings and loan 
associations and savings banks, acting as the operat- 
ing head of FSLIC, enforcing compliance by such 
institutions with regulatory capital requirements, and 
structuring and approving merger and acquisition 
agreements for failing thrifts. FHLBB delegated 
certain of these functions to various Federal Home 


136 


Loan Banks throughout the country, subject to 
FHLBB’s supervision. Under Section 401 of FIR- 
REA, FHLBB was abolished on October 8, 1989; its 
functions and responsibilities, for purposes relevant to 
this action, have been transferred to the Director, 
OTS. 

7. Prior to enactment of FIRREA, FSLIC was an 
agency of the United States established under the 
National Housing Act of 1934, as amended, for the 
purposes of, among other things, insuring the ac- 
counts of depositors in thrift institutions that are 
members of the Federal Home Loan Bank System, 
establishing and enforcing minimum levels of capital 
for insured institutions, enforcing compliance there- 
with, and structuring and approving merger and 
acquisition agreements for failing thrifts. It was 
operated under the direction of the FHLBB. Under 
Section 401 of FIRREA, FSLIC was abolished on 
August 9, 1989, and its responsibilities and functions, 
for purposes relevant to this action, were transferred 
to the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation 
(“FDIC”). 

8. FDIC is an agency of the United States 
established under the Federal Deposit Insurance Act, 
as amended, for the purpose of, among other things, 
insuring the accounts of depositors in certain state 
and federal depository institutions. FDIC is desig- 
nated under FIRREA as the successor in interest to 
FSLIC for purposes relevant to this action. FIRREA 
provides that no action or other proceeding against 
FSLIC shall abate by reason of FIRREA’s enactment 
but that FDIC shall be substituted as its successor in 
interest. 

9. OTS is a bureau of the United States Depart- 
ment of the Treasury, and the Director of OTS is an 
officer and agent of the United States. Under 


137 


FIRREA the Director is charged with regulating and 
supervising all federally insured savings and loan 
associations and savings banks and enforcing com- 
pliance by such institutions with the regulatory 
capital requirements to be established under section 
301 of FIRREA. The Director has been designated 
under FIRREA as the successor in interest to 
FHLBB for purposes relevant to this action. FIR- 
REA provides that no action or other proceeding 
against FHLBB shall abate by reason of FIRREA’s 
enactment but that the Director shall be substituted 
as its successor in interest. 


JURISDICTION 


10. This court has jurisdiction over the subject 
matter of this action pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 1491. 


BACKGROUND 


11. During the late 1970’s and early 1980’s unprece- 
dented high interest rates pushed the thrift indus- 
try’s cost of retaining and obtaining new deposits 
substantially above the rate of return on long-term, 
fixed-rate mortgages in thrift portfolios, precipitating 
a financial crisis in the thrift industry. 

12. As a result of the substantial negative spread 
between the thrift industry’s cost of funds and its 
return on assets, many FSLIC-insured thrift institu- 
tions either became insolvent or were threatened 
with insolvency in the early 1980’s, and FSLIC’s 
insurance fund was faced with the potential loss of 
many billions of dollars in claims by depositors of 
these insolvent or troubled thrifts. For example, 


138 


during the last half of 1981 and the first half of 1982, 
more than 80 percent of all FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions incurred operating losses. 

13. Under the National Housing Act of 1934, as 
amended, FSLIC was at all times relevant to this 
action authorized to facilitate, “in its sole discretion” 
and “upon such terms and conditions as the Corpora- 
tion may determine,” the merger of a failing FSLIC- 
insured thrift institution into a financially sound 
FSLIC-insured thrift institution by providing finan- 
cial assistance in the form of promissory notes 
(“FSLIC notes”) or cash contributions to the healthy 
institution or by guaranteeing it against loss by 
reason of the merger, so long as the cost of the 
financis! assistance provided by FSLIC did not 
exceed the expense that would have been incurred in 
liquidating the institution and paying insurance claims 
upon its accounts. 

14. Faced with a large number of failing thrift in- 
stitutions, FHLBB adopted and aggressively imple- 
mented a policy of requiring insolvent or troubled 
thrift institutions to merge into financially sound 
acquirer institutions. With respect to many such 
“supervisory” mergers, FSLIC exercised its au- 
thority under the National Housing Act to provide 
assistance in the form of cash contributions, FSLIC 
notes, and/or guarantees to induce financially sound 
acquirer institutions to merge with insolvent or 
troubled thrifts. 

15. As the crisis in the thrift industry continued to 
worsen, and more and more FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions became insolvent or troubled, FSLIC and 
FHLBB recognized that continuing to facilitate 
supervisory mergers with cash contributions and 
FSLIC notes would quickly and entirely exhaust the 
insurance fund. Indeed, if FSLIC had provided cash 


139 


contributions and FSLIC notes at historic levels in 
order to induce financially healthy acquirer institu- 
tions to enter into supervisory mergers during 1981 
and 1982, the FSLIC insurance fund, according to 
FSLIC’s own calculations, would have been ex- 
hausted by April of 1982. 

16. In mid-1981, FSLIC and FHLBB were thus 
forced to revise their policies and practices regarding 
the provision of financial assistance in supervisory 
mergers in order to prevent, or at least forestall, 
exhaustion of the FSLIC insurance fund. As de- 
scribed below, because FSLIC and FHLBB could no 
longer provide the amount of cash or other tangible 
financial assistance necessary to induce healthy 
acquirer institutions to merge with insolvent or 
troubled FSLIC-insured thrifts, FSLIC and FHLBB 
began inducing and arranging supervisory mergers by 
providing assistance in the form of an intangible 
asset—“supervisory goodwill.” FSLIC and FHLBB 
also expanded the number of potential acquirers of 
insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured thrift institu- 
tions by permitting interstate mergers (in the 
absence of an acceptable merger proposal from a 
financially sound in-state acquirer institution), and 
inter-industry mergers (in the absence of an accept- 
able merger proposal from either an in-state or out- 
of-state thrift institution). In evaluating merger 
proposals, FSLIC’s principal objective was to merge 
the insolvent or troubled thrift institution into a 
healthy acquirer at the lowest possible cost to 
FSLIC, 

_ 17. In order to induce financially sound acquirer 
institutions to merge with insolvent or failing FSLIC- 
insured thrifts with little or no cash contributions, 
FSLIC notes, or other tangible financial assistance 
from FSLIC, FSLIC and FHLBB relied primarily 


140 


upon an aggressive application of the “purchase 
method” of accounting for the mergers. ‘Ine Generally 
Accepted Accounting Principles (“GAAP”) applicable 
at the time provided that in mergers and acquisitions 
using the purchase method of accounting, the book 
value of the assets and liabilities of an acquired thrift 
institution should be adjusted (“marked to market”) 
to fair market value at the time of the acquisition, 
and that any excess in the cost of the acquisition 
(which includes any liabilities assumed by the ac- 
quirer) over the fair market value of the acquired 
assets should be separately recorded on the acquirer 
institution’s books as “goodwill”—an intangible, non- 
earning asset that may be amortized on a straight- 
line basis over a period of up to 40 years. _ 

18. Because of the high interest rates prevailing at 
the time, the fair market value of the lower yielding 
fixed-rate mortgage loans held by most acquired 
institutions was significantly less than the mortgage 
loans’ book value (the face amount of. the loans). 
Accordingly, in the typical supervisory merger at that 
time, the aggregate fair market value of the liabilities 
assumed by the acquirer institution exceeded the 
aggregate fair market value of the acquired institu- 
tion’s fixed-rate loans and other assets. The amount 
by which the acquired institution’s liabilities exceed 
its assets is reflected on the merged institution’s 
books as goodwill. Goodwill recorded in connection 
with a supervisory merger is known as “supervisory 
goodwill.” 

19. Certain mergers accomplished under the pur- 
chase method of accounting involved the “push down” 
technique. In a merger accounted for under the push 
down method, the assets and liabilities of the 
acquired institution are marked to market, and the 
difference plus the acquiring institution’s merger 


141 


costs are separately allocated to goodwill on the 
books of the resulting institution. According to 
GAAP, goodwill generated from a merger accounted 
for under this method may be amortized to expense 
by the interest method over a period no longer than 
that over which the discount on the interest-bearing 
assets acquired is to be recognized as interest income. 

20. Supervisory goodwill was recorded on the 
merged institution’s balance sheet as an asset 
includable in capital under GAAP and under 
FHLBB’s regulations governing the minimum capital 
requirements of FSLIC-insured thrift institutions. 
The ability to include supervisory goodwill as capital 
for purposes of satisfying FHLBB’s minimum capital 
requirements and to amortize it over a lengthy period 
was essential to inducing healthy acquirer institutions 
to merge with insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured 
thrifts. By assuring acquirer institutions in super- 
visory mergers that supervisory goodwill would be 
treated in the above-described manner for regulatory 
purposes, FSLIC and FHLBB were able to induce 
and arrange supervisory mergers with minimal cash 
contributions and other tangible financial assistance. 
FSLIC and FHLBB thus saved many billions of 
dollars in financial assistance that FSLIC would 
otherwise have had to pay in order to resolve scores 
of insolvent and troubled FSLIC-insured thrift 
institutions. 

21. As part of its effort to induce and arrange 
supervisory mergers by using supervisory goodwill in 
the above-described manner, FHLBB took steps to 
assure the thrift industry that GAAP would control in 
determining the application of purchase method 
accounting and the treatment of supervisory goodwill 
in supervisory mergers. In August 1981, FHLBB 
withdrew a proposed rule change that would have 


142 


imposed regulatory standards more stringent than 
GAAP for accounting for supervisory goodwill. The 
proposed rule would have limited the inclusion of 
supervisory goodwill in regulatory capital to 30 
percent of the merged institution’s net worth and 
would have required that supervisory goodwill be 
amortized over a period no longer than ten years. In 
withdrawing the proposed rule, the FHLBB stated 
that a principal reason for its action was that the 
proposal could “discourage potential merger partners 
from consummating necessary mergers.” In Septem- 
ber 1981 FHLBB issued Memorandum R31b, which 


made clear that, as a matter of FHLBB ,olicy, GAAP 


would control in determining the application of 
purchase method accounting and the treatment of 
supervisory goodwill in supervisory mergers. 

22. FSLIC also realized savings over the cost of 
liquidating failed thrifts through the creation of 
“FSLIC capital contributions,” a regulatory capital 
credit. If the amount of tangible financial assistance 
provided by FSLIC in connection with a supervisory 
merger was insufficient to restore the insolvent 
thrift’s balance sheet to minimum regulatory capital 
levels, the acquisition would have a negative effect on 
the capital position of the acquirer institution. In 
order to eliminate or reduce this negative effect, 
while holding the cost of assistance and cash outlays 
to a minimum, FSLIC and FHLBB assured certain 
acquirer institutions that FSLIC financial assistance, 
or some portion thereof, would be treated as 
regulatory capital, recordable on the resulting institu- 
tion’s balance sheet for regulatory reporting purposes 
as a direct credit to capital under FHLBB’s regula- 
tions governing the minimum capital requirements of 
FSLIC-insured thrift institutions. This practice devi- 
ated from GAAP, which required that such cash 


143 


contributions be charged against (and thus reduce) 
any goodwill arising out of the transaction. The 
ability to include these contributions as capital for 
purposes of satisfying FHLBB’s minimum capital 
requirements was in some cases essential tu inducing 
a healthy acquirer institution to merge with an 
insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured thrift on the 
terms proposed by FSLIC and FHLBB. By assuring 
acquirer institutions in supervisory mergers that 
some or all of the FSLIC assistance would be treated 
in the above-described manner for regulatory report- 
ing purposes, FSLIC and FHLBB were able to 
induce and arrange supervisory mergers with minimal 
cash payments and other tangible financial assistance. 
FSLIC and FHLBB thus saved large sums of 
financial assistance that FSLIC would otherwise have 
had to pay in order to resolve many insolvent and 
troubled FSLIC insured thrift institutions. 

23. During the last 7 months of 1981, FSLIC 
provided tangible financial assistance (e.g. cash, 
FSLIC notes) in connection with the merger of 23 
insolvent or troubled FSLIC-insured thrift institu- 
tions with assets totaling $12.3 billion. These 23 
supervisory mergers were arranged at a present 
value cost to FSLIC of $663 million, or 18 percent of 
the estimated cost (at present value) of liquidation. 
Similarly, in 1982 FSLIC provided financial assistance 
in connection with 49 supervisory mergers involving 
77 FSLIC-insured insolvent or troubled thrifts with 
assets totaling $31.2 billion. The present value cost to 
FSLIC of these supervisory mergers was $1.2 billion, 
or 16.3 percent of the estimated cost of liquidation. In 
effect, by using supervisory goodwill in accounting for 
supervisory mergers under the purchase method, 
FSLIC transferred much of its cost of resolving 
insolvent or failing thrifts to the healthy members of 


144 


the industry and their shareholders. As then- 
Chairman Pratt stated in March 1982: “(Bly using 
mergers as a supervisory tool ..., we are seeking to 
spread as much of the industry’s own net worth as 
possible over the industry’s trouble spots as a means 
of conserving the FSLIC’s insurance fund. 

24. In 1982 the Financial Accounting Standards 
Board, which promulgates GAAP, issued Statement 
of Financial Accounting Standards No. 72 (“FASB 
72”), which amended GAAP applicable to mergers 
initiated after September 30, 1982. i 72 = 
ally requires that goodwill be amortized over a perio 
of Ae se than previously permitted by GAAP. This 
change in GAAP did not alter FHLBB's and FSLIC’s 
regulatory policies and practices with regard to 
supervisory goodwill. For regulatory accounting pur- 
poses, FHLBB and FSLIC continued to induce 
healthy institutions to acquire failing thrifts by 
allowing goodwill to be included in capital and 
amortized over a period of time exceeding that now 
allowed by GAAP. | : 

25. The nature of the problems affecting the thrift 
industry changed in 1983-1984. After mid-1982, mar- 
ket interest rates declined, thus improving the 
“negative spread” that had plagued the industry. But 
in 1983 and 1984 thrifts increasingly faced problems 
with deteriorating assets. This asset quality problem 
threatened many thrift institutions that were just 
recovering from the negative spread problems of 
1981-82. Thirty-five percent of all FSLIC-insured 
thrifts lost money in the second half of 1983. 

26. Although economic conditions improved in 1983, 
the number of problem cases and the volume of assets 
handled by FSLIC in default prevention transactions 
continued at record levels in that year. FSLIC 
resolved 53 problem cases involving $17.6 billion in 


145 


assets in 1983. This marked a decrease from 77 
FSLIC resolved cases in 1982, but the complexity of 
the problem cases increased significantly. FSLIC 
realized significant cost savings by resolving most of 
these problem cases through interstate and inter- 
industry acquisitions. Through aggressive use of 
purchase method accounting, FSLIC saved an esti- 
mated $2.4 billion over the cost of liquidation in 
resolving these problem cases. 

27. Overall thrift industry profitability declined in 
1984, despite the fact that interest rates were far 
below the 1981-1982 levels. In 1984 FSLIC resolved 
27 problem cases involving $5.5 billion in assets. The 
continued growth in the size and complexity of 
problem cases requiring FSLIC intervention, how- 
ever, created a record-high demand on FSLIC 
resources, precipitating a decline in FSLIC reserves 
from $6.4 billion at the end of 1983 to approximately 
$5.6 billion at the end of 1984. During 1984, despite 
FSLIC’s efforts to obtain less costly resolutions, 
FSLIC was forced to place 9 institutions into 
liquidating receiverships. The other 18 problem cases 
were resolved by FSLIC at approximately 45 percent 
of the estimated cost of liquidation, or a savings of 
$693 million, largely through the use of supervisory 
goodwill to induce healthy institutions to take over 
the failing thrifts. 

28. In 1985 the size and complexity of cases 
requiring FSLIC assistance kept the demand on 
FSLIC resources at record levels. The FSLIC 
insurance fund was being depleted so quickly that 
FHLBB in 1985 levied quarterly special insurance 
premiums on insured institutions, adding more than 
$1 billion to the insurance fund. Even with the 
extraordinary assessment, FSLIC’s total insurance 
reserves in 1985 decreased more than $1 billion, to 


146 


$4.6 billion. FSLIC resolved 35 problem cases in 1985 
involving total assets of $6.5 billion. Twenty-five of 
these cases involved assisted acquisitions represent- 
ing $4.4 billion in total assets. Through techniques 
such as interstate, inter-industry, and private- 
investor acquisitions, the 25 assisted acquisitions 
saved FSLIC $547 million over the estimated cost of 
liquidation. During 1985, FSLIC appointed a liquidat- 
ing receiver for 10 institutions. Because of the 
continuing high case load and associated losses, 
FSLIC’s need for capital remained intense at the end 
of 1985. 

29. The intense need for FSLIC capital became 
urgent in 1986, when the total reserves for FSLIC 
diminished by year end to negative $6.3 billion. 
FHLBB’s overriding concern during 1986 was the 
development of a plan to recapitalize the FSLIC 
insurance fund. In 1986 the gap between profitable 
thrifts and unprofitable ones widened, with approx- 
imately one-fifth of the institutions experiencing 
accelerating losses. FHLBB and FSLIC resolved 27 
problem thrifts with assisted mergers and acquisi- 
tions in 1986, involving $7.4 billion in assets. FSLIC 
liquidated 10 institutions in 1986. 

30. In 1987, the thrift industry’s financial condition 
continued to deteriorate, as the industry reported an 
overall net loss of $6.8 billion. FSLIC’s financial 
problems also worsened and, by the end of 1987, its 
reserves had plummeted to a negative $13.7 billion as 
it incurred operating losses of $8.6 billion for the 
year. Of the 48 FSLIC cases resolved in 1987 at an 
estimated cost of $4.5 billion, 31 were resolved by 
assisted sales, saving FSLIC $400 million over the 
cost of liquidation. While FSLIC’s capitalization 
problems were somewhat eased in 1987 by the 
passage of the Competitive Equality Banking Act of 


147 


1987 (“CEBA”), which authorized the sale of bonds to 
recapitalize the FSLIC, by the end of 1987 there 
were still 259 problem institutions in the FSLIC 
caseload. The estimated cost of resolving these was 
$17.4 billion. The General Accounting Office estimated 
that an additional 300 institutions with assets of about 
$88 billion were insolvent under GAAP standards 
thereby exposing FSLIC to further risk. 
31. Recognizing this, the FHLBB continued to seek 
ways in which to facilitate supervisory acquisitions. 
For example, even though CEBA required generally 
that thrift institutions conform their accounting 
practices to GAAP, the FHLBB issued accounting 
regulations implementing CEBA which provided that 
accounting forbearances would continue to be in- 
cluded as a component of regulatory capital. 53 Fed. 
Reg. 324 (January 6, 1988). The rationale for the 
continued infusion of accounting forbearances was 
that such forbearances “have often been required to 
rong acquisition of a troubled institution....” 
32. By the end of 1988, FSLIC’s defici 
increased to $75 billion. It incurred a net magh 906 
billion for the year, and the thrift industry’s net 
losses, at $13 billion, were almost double the year 
before. While FSLIC reported a $43.6 billion loss 
allowance for the estimated cost of resolving 578 
problem thrifts that had not been resolved in 1988, it 
was able to dispose of 205 insolvent thrifts and 
stabilize 18 others by supplementing its CEBA bond 
receipts with FSLIC notes and long-term assistance 
agreements to attract acquirers of insolvent institu- 
tions. As FSLIC did not have the additional billions 
in cash to liquidate insolvent institutions and to pay 
off depositors, FSLIC arranged less expensive trans- 
actions with acquirer institutions through the use of 


148 


assistance agreements continuing provisions to cover 
the negative net worth of failed thrifts and to cover 


losses on nonperforming or poor quality assets. 


THE TRANSACTIONS 


33. On March 11, 1988, plaintiffs, through the newly 
formed Statesman Bank, purchased substantially all 
of the assets and assumed substantially all of the 
liabilities of First Federated Savings Bank, of West 
Palm Beach, Florida (“Federated”), from FSLIC, 
which had recently been appointed receiver for 
Federated. On that same date, March 11, 1988, 
Statesman Bank merged with First Federal Savings 
Bank of Waterloo Iowa (“First Federal”), Peoples 
Savings and Loan Association of Waterloo lowa 
(“Peoples”), and The Perpetual Savings and Loan 
Association of Waterloo, lowa (“Perpetual”). First 
Federal, Peoples, and Perpetual are sometimes here- 
inafter referred to as the “Waterloo Institutions.” 
The acquisitions all took place in a supervisory 
transaction induced and arranged by FSLIC and 
FHLBB. 

34. Federated was a stock savings bank organized 
under the laws of Florida, the accounts of which were 
insured by FSLIC. For the year ended December 31, 
1987, Federated reported a net loss of over $7 million. 
This reported loss did not include various audit 
adjustments made during the year for additional 
charge-offs of approximately $32 million. By Resolu- 
tion No. 88-167 dated March 11, 1988, FHLBB 
concluded that Federated was insolvent and ap- 
pointed FSLIC receiver for Federated contingent 
upon receipt of approval from the Comptroller, 
Bureau of Thrift, Savings and Loan Section, of the 


149 


State of Florida pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 1729. The 
Comptroller provided such approval, and FSLIC was 
— receiver for First Federated that same 

35. Prior to the merger, Perpetual was a mutual 
thrift chartered under the laws of Iowa, the accounts 
of which were insured by FSLIC. For the year ended 
December 31, 1987, Perpetual reported a net loss of 
$4.2 million. By Resolution No. 88-168 dated March 
il, 1988, FHLBB concluded that Perpetual was 
insolvent and appointed FSLIC conservator for 
Perpetual contingent upon receipt of approval from 
the Superintendent of Savings and Loans of the State 
of lowa pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 1729. The Superintend- 
ent of Savings and Loans of Iowa provided such 
approval, and FSLIC was appointed conservator for 
Perpetual that same date. 

36. Prior to the merger, First Federal was a 
federally chartered mutual savings bank, the accounts 
of which were insured by FSLIC. For the year ended 
December 31, 1987, First Federal reported a net loss 
of $12.5 million. At the time of its merger into 
“oe y oa aber Federal was insolvent. 

. Prior to the merger, Peoples was a federal 
chartered mutual savings and a association, the 
accounts of which were insured by FSLIC. For the 
year ended December 31, 1987, Peoples reported net 
loss of $4.4 million. At the time of its merger into 
Statesman Bank, Peoples was insolvent. 

38. In 1987, the Statesman Group explored the 
possibility of acquiring a subsidiary of Federated with 
assistance from FSLIC. In discussions with FSLIC 
thereafter, FSLIC “packaged” Federated with the 
three Waterloo Institutions, and requested bids from 


others for the potential acquisition of the four 
institutions. 


150 


39. On January 8, 1988, plaintiffs submitted a 
proposal (“Proposal”) to acquire the four institutions. 
The Proposal made clear that plaintiffs were not 
content to rely on FHLBB’s policies regarding certain 


aspects of the transaction, as it specified many of the - 


conditions of the proposed acquisitions. The Proposal 
called for the formation and capitalization of SSHC 
and Statesman Bank. The initial capital infusion by 
plaintiffs into SSHC was to be $21 million, of which 
$12.6 million was to be contributed by Statesman 
Group in the form of Statesman Group preferred 
stock, and $8.4 million was to be cash contributed by 
American Life. SSHC was to infuse all of the 
preferred stock and cash into Statesman Bank. 
Statesman Bank was to be organized and chartered 
by FSLIC, and then turned over to SSHC to be a 
wholly-owned subsidiary of SSHC. The Proposal 
called for assistance from FSLIC in the fore of cash 
and a promissory note in the total amount of the 
combined deficit of the four failed associations, with 
adjustments. FSLIC was also to guarantee partial 
reimbursement of losses on certain Covered Assets, 
to provide payments for spread and yield mainte- 
nance on certain covered assets, and to provide 
indemnification against certain losses. 

40. The Proposal also spelled out certain regulatory 
considerations and forbearances. First, the Proposal 
made clear that the purchase method of accounting 
was to be used, and that $21 million of the FSLIC 
cash contribution was to be a “capital credit” and 
would thus become a permanent part of Statesman 
Bank’s regulatory net worth. Specifically the Proposal 
stated: 


For regulatory purposes, with respect to the 
books and records of [Statesman Bank], push- 


151 


down accounting shall be used to record the 
purchase of [Statesman Bank’s] capital stock by 
SSHC. Twenty-one million of the cash contribu- 
tion to be made to [Statesman Bank] pursuant to 
an assistance agreement to be entered into 
between the FSLIC and Statesman [Group] is to 
be a credit to [Statesman Bank’s] regulatory 
capita; therefore, for regulatory accounting pur- 
poses, {Statesman Bank] will book such contribu- 
tion as a direct addition to its net worth. 
Accordingly, immediately after the acquisition, 
the regulatory capital of (Statesman Bank] will 
be $42,000,000, which will consist of $21,000,000 in 
capital infused by SSHC, plus $21,000,000 in 
capital contributed by the FSLIC which will be 
treated as regulatory capital. Not later than 30 
days following the acquisition, [Statesman Bank] 
shall submit to the Supervisory Agent an opinion 
of independent certified public accountants that 
[Statesman Bank] has accounted for the transac- 
tion in accordance with generally accepted ac- 
counting principles (“GAAP”) including the use of 
“push down” accounting, except that as provided 
by the Board, for purposes of reporting to the 
Board, twenty-one million dollars of the cash 
contribution by the FSLIC to [Statesman Bank] 
pursuant to the assistance agreement shall be 
deemed a contribution to regulatory net worth 
and will be booked as a direct credit to 
[Statesman Bank]. 


41. The Proposal called for additional regulatory 
considerations and forbearances, including a regula- 
tory capital maintenance agreemeni, waiver of ceriain 
debt restrictions that may apply to Statesman Group, 
authorization of certain cross-marketing activities by 


152 


plaintiffs, and other considerations and forbearances. 
Many of these considerations and forbearances were 
required to provide for waivers of regulatory restric- 
tions on companies such as Statesman Group and 
American Life, which were engaged in the insurance 
business, to acquire a controlling interest in a thrift 
institution. All regulatory forbearances, including the 
insistence of purchase method accounting and the re- 
quirement of the permanent capital credit, were 
integral to the acquisitions and without which the 
transactions would have been nonsensical. 

42. Attached as Appendix E to the Proposal was a 
letter from the independent accounting firm of Ernst 
and Whinney, which concluded that, “the proposed 
use of the purchase method of accounting will be in 
accordance with [GAAP].” The letter went on to state 
that the Statesman Group’s “proposal presents the 
$25 million excess of liabilities assumed over the 
estimated fair value of assets acquired as an 
unidentified intangible,” [i.e., goodwill]. 

43. The Proposal incorporated by reference the 
plaintiffs’ Business Plan. The Business Plan made 
clear that the purchase method of accounting was 
integral to the proposal, as it stated that, “[t]he 
projected financial statements have been prepared 
with purchase accounting being applied by use of the 
‘Push-Down’ method. Inasmuch as the acquisition 
does not give rise to continuity- of ownership, 
Statesman believes that application of the purchase 
method is appropriate and is in accordance with 
[GAAP}.” The Business Plan also expressly stated 
that it was “based upon the assumption that certain 
regulatory forbearances and considerations will be 
granted,” and stressed that the forbearances were 
“necessary to consummate the transaction.” The 
Business Plan contained projected statements of 


153 


condition, which indicate that the $21 million of the 
cash contribution from FSLIC was to be treated as 
permanent regulatory capital of Statesman Bank and 
not subject to reduction through amortization. The 
notes to the projections made clear that the purchase 
method of secounting was a major assumption on 
which the entire proposal was based: 


(3) Accounting for the Acquisition 


The accompanying projected financial state- 
ments have been prepared assuming purchase 
accounting is applied to record the acquisition of 
the Bank and that purchase accounting is applied 
by the “push down” method as of December 31, 
1987. Purchase accounting is the method of 
recording a business acquisition by adjusting 
assets and liabilities to market value and 
eliminating net worth. “Push-down” refers to 
recording the adjustments on the books of the 
acquired company. 


The Business Plan expressly contemplated that 
Statesman Bank would record on its books “(gloodwill 
of approximately $25.8 million arising from the 
acquisition. ...” 

44. Plaintiffs also submitted to FSLIC an Applica- 
tion H-(e)l1 (“Application”) for approval of the 
acquisition of the banks, dated February 12, 1988. 
The Application incorporated the Proposal and the 
Business Plan. In additional, the Application made 
clear that, “the Acquisition Proposal is subject to a 
number specific terms and conditions, among which 
are certain regulatory forbearances and considera- 
tions to be granted or obtained by the FSLIC.... 
Applicant wishes to emphasize the importance of the 
requested forbearances and considerations to the 


154 


completion of the Acquisition....” The Application also 
summarized the specified conditions, stressing that, 


[flor regulatory purposes with respect to the 
books and records of [Statesman Bank], push- 
down accounting shall be used to record the 
purchase of [Statesman Bank’s] capital stock by 
the Holding Company. Twenty-one million dollars 
of the cash contribution to be made to [Statesman 
Bank] pursuant to an assistance agreement to be 
entered into between the FSLIC and Applicant is 
to be a credit to !Statesman Bank’s] regulatory 
capital; therefore, for regulatory accounting pur- 
poses, [Statesman Bank] will book such contribu- 
tion as a direct addition to its net worth. 


The Application did not even refer to the possibility 
that the $21 million capital credit would be amortized. 
45. The Application also reiterated several other 
regulatory considerations and forbearances that were 
integral to the transaction. On March 1, 1988, 
plaintiffs amended their Application H-(e)1 in certain 
respects, but did not amend any aspect of the 
proposal affecting the use of the purchase-method 
accounting or of the $21 million capital credit. 

46. The purchase method of accounting and the 
capital credit for $21 million of the FSLIC cash 
contribution were integral to plaintiffs’ bid. Had 
plaintiffs not been permitted to record as capital the 
supervisory goodwill in the amount by which the 
acquired institution’s liabilities exceeded their assets 
in addition to the $21,000,000 of FSLIC assistance, 
plaintiffs’ acquisition of the institutions on the 
financial terms proposed would have rendered the 
entire transaction senseless. Had Statesman Bank not 
been permitted to record supervisory goodwill and 


155 


the $21 million of FSLIC assistance as regulatory 
capital, it would have been considered insolvent for 
regulatory purposes on the date of the acquisition, 
and thus immediately subject to liquidation by 
FHLBB and FSLIC. Plaintiffs would not have made 
such a proposal. Nor would they have made a 
proposal which did not include the other requested 
regulatory considerations and forbearances. Thus, 
plaintiffs’ willingness to enter into the supervisory 
transaction on the financial terms set forth in the 
Proposal was absolutely conditioned on FHLBB’s and 
FSLIC’s agreement to permit plaintiffs to report 
supervisory goodwill as capital, and to report the $21 
million of FSLIC assistance as a permanent credit to 
the capital of Statesman Bank for regulatory pur- 
poses. Absent all of the specified regulatory consid- 
erations and forbearances, plaintiffs either would 
have insisted upon receiving far greater tangible 
financial assistance from FSLIC, such as additional 
cash contributions or promissory notes, or would have 
withdrawn their offer. 

47. FSLIC and FHLBB determined that plaintiffs’ 
bid was the most attractive solution for the four 
insolvent institutions. The parties negotiated and 
agreed upon several of the terms and conditions of 
the transaction, set forth below. After FSLIC and 
FHLBB selected plaintiffs’ bid, FHLBB and FSLIC 
organized and chartered Statesman Bank, and caused 
it to issue common stock to SSHC at closing. 
Statesman Bank entered into a separate Merger 
Agreement and Plan of Merger (“Merger Agree- 
ment”) with each of the three Waterloo Institutions. 
Each of the Merger Agreements was expressly 
conditioned upon the execution of an Assistance 
Agreement among FSLIC, Statesman Bank, States- 
man Group, American Life, and SSHC. 


156 


48. Statesman Bank also entered into an Acquisi- 
tion Agreement with FSLIC as receiver for Fede- 
rated, under which Statesman Bank agreed to 
purchase substantially all of the assets, and to assume 
substantially all of the liabilities, of Federated. The 
Acquisition Agreement stated that computations 
made for purposes of the acquisition would be 
governed by GAAP, except that where GAAP 
conflicted with any action, regulations or resolutions 
of FHLBB and FSLIC, then the action, regulation or 
resolution would prevail over GAAP. The Acquisition 
Agreement also incorporated FHLBB Resolutions 
adopted contemporaneously with the Acquisition 
Agreement. 

49. FSLIC and all of the plaintiffs entered into an 
Assistance Agreement pertaining to the transaction. 
Under the Assistance Agreement, FSLIC agreed to 
contribute $60 million to Statesman Bank. The 
Assistance Agreement states that $26 million of the 
$60 million cash contribution from FSLIC was to be 
considered part of Statesman Bank’s regulatory 
capital. In addition, under the Assistance Agreement, 
FSLIC delivered a Promissory Note in the amount of 
the difference between the acquired associations’ 
negative regulatory capital and the $60 million cash 
provided by FSLIC. Also under the Assistance 
Agreement, Statesman Bank delivered to FSLIC a 
Subordinated Debenture in the face amount of $5 
million at a term of ten years. The Assistance 
Agreement provided that it “shall be governed by 
GAAP, except that where such principles conflict 
with the terms of the Agreement, applicable regula- 
tions of the Bank Board or [FSLIC], or any resolution 
or action of the Bank Board approving or relating to 
the Acquisition, the Mergers or to this Agreement, 
then this Agreement, such regulations, or such 


157 


resolution or action shall govern.” The Assistance 
Agreement also provided for continuing FSLIC 
assistance on certain Covered Assets on which 
FSLIC was to assume 93 percent of losses. 

50. Also on March 11, 1988, plaintiffs entered into a 
Regulatory Capital Maintenance Agreement with 
FSLIC, under which plaintiffs agreed that Statesman 
Bank would maintain the regulatory capital of the 
Bank in compliance with regulatory capital require- 
ments. Section 2 of that Agreement defined regula- 
tory capital to include “amounts permitted by the 
FSLIC in the Assistance Agreement and in the 
forbearances issued in connection with the transac- 
tions discussed herein.” The Regulatory Capital 
Maintenance Agreement provided that the computa- 
tions of regulatory capital “shall be perpetual.” Under 
that Agreement, Statesman Group and American Life 
guaranteed to infuse up to $4 million of capital into 
Statesman Bank and SSHC as necessary to maintain 
Statesman Bank’s regulatory capital at required 
levels. Also, under the Regulatory Capital Mainte- 
nance Agreement plaintiffs granted FSLIC voting 
rights for the stock of SSHC, to be exercised in the 
event that Statesman Bank failed to satisfy minimum 
regulatory capital requirements. 

51. The Assistance Agreement and the Regulatory 
Capital Maintenance Agreement incorporated the 
Resolutions of the Bank Board issued in connection 
with the acquisitions. One of the Resolutions issued 
by FHLBB, Resolution No. 88-169, recited that the 
transfer of the assets and liabilities of Federated as 
provided for in the Acquisition Agreement “is the 
most desirable alternative, consistent with Bank 
Board and FSLIC policy, to the liquidation of 
[Federated] and the payment of insurance on its 
accounts....” The Resolution also determined that 


158 


each of the acquired associations was a “failing 
institution,” that the acquisition of Federated and the 
mergers of the Waterloo Institutions “would lessen 
the risk to the FSLIC,” that “no other offer was 
made to acquire all of the Associations,” that there 
was no other qualified offeror who presented an 
acceptable offer “the estimated cost of which was 
within the lesser of 15 percent or $15 million of the 
cost of the offer of the Applicants to acquire all of the 
associations,” that plaintiffs’ bid “presents the lowest 
expense and least risk to the FSLIC of any proposal 
submitted for any of the Associations,” and that “the 
offer of the Applicants is substantially less costly to 
the FSLIC than any alternative proposal or pro- 
posals.” FHLBB further found that “financial assist- 
ance and indemnification by the FSLIC are necessary 
to prevent the default of each Association and to 
facilitate the Acquisition and the Mergers.” 

52. The Resolution approved the Assistance Agree- 
ment, the Promissory Note, the Subordinated Deben- 
ture, and the forbearances from regulatory 
requirements. 

53. With respect to the acquisition of Federated’s 
assets and liabilities and the mergers of the Waterloo 
Institutions, FHLBB resolved that Statesman Bank 
shall report to FHLBB and FSLIC that the transac- 
tions were accounted for in accordance with GAAP as 
interpreted by FHLBB and FSLIC regulations, but 
specifically noted two departures from GAAP: (a) 
that the $21 million contribution by FSLIC and the 
$5 million principal amount of the Subordinated 
Debenture issued to FSLIC “shall be credited to the 
regulatory capital account of’ Statesman Bank; and 
(b) that goodwill could be amortized over a period of 
up to 25 years. FHLBB further resolved that 
Statesman Bank shall furnish an independent certified 


159 


accountant’s opinion within 90 days following the 
effective date which shall “indicate that the Acquisi- 
tion and the Mergers were consummated in accord- 


ance with [GAAP] except as otherwise authori 
the Bank Board,” and : aoe 


which shall (a) specifically describe, as of the

goodwill and the discount and premiums arising 
from the Acquisition and the mergers, to be

attributed to intangible assets, including goodwill

amortization periods and methods.... 


54. At closing FHLBB issued to Statesman Bank a 
forbearance letter which set forth eleven enumerated 


- regulatory forbearances that had been negotiated and 


agreed upon by plaintiffs and FHLBB, and which are 
part of the parties’ contractual commitments. The 
forbearance letter included FHLBB’s agreement (a) 
not to enforce for five years regulatory capital 
deficiencies arising from specified items, including the 
acquired associations’ liabilities and net worth defi- 
ciency, and (b) to include $21 million of FSLIC’s 
contribution, and the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture, in Statesman Bank’s regula- 
tory capital. 

55. In accordance with the agreements of the 
parties, at closing FSLIC issued to Statesman Bank a 
promissory note of $43 million which has subse- 
quently been adjusted to a principal amount of over 
$55 million. Statesman Bank also issued to FSLIC a 


Subordinated Debenture in the face amount of $5 
million. 


160 


56. The purchase of Federated’s assets and the 
assumption of its liabilities, and the supervisory 
mergers of the Waterloo Institutions, were all 
consummated on March 11, 1988 in accordance with 
the terms and conditions set forth in the items 
described above. 

57. In reliance upon FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s ex- 
press contractual agreement concerning the use of 
purchase-method accounting, the acquired assets and 
liabilities of Federated, Perpetual, Peoples, and First 
Federal were adjusted to fair market value at the 
time of the transactions, and the resulting excess of 
liabilities over acquired assets was recorded on 
Statesman Bank’s books as an intangible asset to be 
included in capital and amortized in accordance with 
FASB 72. The amount of supervisory goodwill 
ultimately recorded by Statesman Bank as a result of 
the acquisitions was $27,874,583. Also in reliance on 
FHLBB’s and FSLIC’s express agreements, States- 
man Bank recorded as regulatory capital the $21 
million of FSLIC assistance and the $5 million face 
amount of the Subordinated Debenture. In financial 
reports filed with FSLIC and FHLBB since the 
transaction, Statesman Bank has consistently re- 
ported these items as assets included in capital for 
regulatory purposes, in accordance with and in 
reliance upon, FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s express agree- 
ment. In accordance with the agreements with 
FSLIC and FHLBB, Statesman Bank has not 
amortized the $21 million capital credit in its reports 
to FHLBB. FHLBB objected to Statesman Bank’s 
failure to amortize the capital credit, despite its 
agreement that the capital credit shall be perpetual. 
Other than this objection, prior to the enactment of 
FIRREA, neither FSLIC nor FHLBB objected to 
Statesman Bank’s regulatory accounting treatment of 
these items. 


161 


58. FLSIC and FHLBB benefited enormously from 
the use as regulatory capital of supervisory goodwill, 
the capital credit of $21 million, and the $5 million 
face amount of the Subordinated Debenture in 
connection with the acquisition of Federated’s lia- 
bilities and assets, and the mergers of the Waterloo 
Institutions. As of December 31, 1987, the four failed 
institutions reported an aggregate negative net worth 
of $90.2 million. If these figures were adjusted to 
reflect the fair market value of the assets and 
liabilities, the difference between the liabilities and 
assets would exceed the aggregate negative net 
worth of the institutions. Indeed, the initial audit 
reports (before the GAAP purchase accounting mark 
to market entries), currently in the process of 
finalization, detail the negative regulatory net worth 
of the four institutions at an amount in excess of $115 
million in the aggregate. When the failed thrifts’ 
assets and liabilities were later marked to market, 
Statesman Bank ultimately recorded the difference as 
supervisory goodwill in the amount of $27,874,583. 
This figure did not include FSLIC’s exposure of 93 
percent of the losses on Covered Assets under the 
Assistance Agreement. If all of the Covered Assets of 
the four failed thrifts had been included in the mark 
to market analysis, the difference between the fair 
market value of the four thrifts’ assets and liabilities 
would have increased by more than $30 million. Thus, 
the cost of liquidating the four institutions would 
have exceeded the sum of (a) the aggregate negative 
regulatory capital of $115 million, (b) the goodwill of 
$27.9 million, and (c) $30 million as the allowance for 
loss on the remaining 93 percent of Covered Assets, 
for a total of $172.9 million. Even this figure is a 
conservative estimate of the cost of liquidation, in 
light of operating losses, deterioration of asset 


162 


quality, deposit outflows, and resolution costs such as 
brokerage fees, holding costs, etc. 

59. Indeed, in a Statement Before the Committee 
on Banking, Housing and Urban Affairs of the United 
States Senate dated May 26, 1988, M. Danny Wall, 
then Chairman of FHLBB, praised many aspects of 
the Statesman transaction, stating that “the financial 
assistance provided by the FSLIC in connection with 
these acquisitions was $50 million less than the cost 
to FSLIC of liquidating the four institutions.” Mr. 
Wall referred to the transaction as “one of the more 
significant acquisitions arranged by FSLIC,” and 
stated that it was “noteworthy not only for the 
number of institutions involved,” but also because 
FHLBB granted forbearances as to cross-marketing 
of products and services between subsidiaries of 
Statesman Group and Statesman Bank. Mr. Wall 
stated that any restrictions on cross-marketing 
“would have been a material impediment” to a 
transaction worth $50 million to the government. 

60. Plaintiffs have complied in good faith with all 
the terms and conditions of the agreements relating 
to the acquisition of the four institutions and have 
otherwise fully performed their undertakings in 
connection therewith. 

61. Plaintiffs’ acquisitions of and investment in 
Federated and the Waterloo Institutions were made 
in reliance upon FHLBB’s and FSLIC’s policies and 
statements encouraging and approving the use of the 
purchase method of accounting and the inclusion of 
goodwill, the $21 million capital credit and the $5 
million face amount of the Subordinated Debenture in 
capital for regulatory reporting purposes. FHLBB 
and FSLIC were fully aware of plaintiffs’ reliance on 
these policies and statements, and well knew or 
should have known that none of the plaintiffs would 


163 


acquire or invest in Federated and the Waterloo 
Institutions absent the specified regulatory account- 
ing treatment of goodwill, the $21 million regulatory 
capital credit, and inclusion of the $5 million face 
amount of the Subordinated Debenture in regulatory 
capital. 

62. The parties’ agreement concerning the use of 
supervisory goodwill, the permanent capital treat- 
ment of the $21 million capital credit, and the 
inclusion of the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture were essential terms and 
conditions of the transaction, and were essential to 
the parties’ purpose of allowing plaintiffs a reasonable 
opportunity to turn the four failed thrifts into a 
profitable venture. Plaintiffs would not have agreed 
to acquire the four failed thrifts in the absence of 
such regulatory accounting treatment, because 
Statesman Bank would have been left insolvent and 
because Statesman Bank would otherwise have been 
unable to manage itself without substantial regulatory 
oversight that would interfere with its business and 


prospects. 


STATESMAN BANK’S POST-MERGER 
PERFORMANCE 


63. Plaintiffs’ acquisition of the four failed institu- 
tions prevented their default and liquidation, thus 
saving the FSLIC insurance fund, by FHLBB’s 
admission, $50 million. Since the acquisition, States- 
man Bank has performed well, achieving the purposes 
of the acquisitions. Statesman Bank has resolved 
problem loans, eliminated non-performing assets, and 
operated soundly. In human terms, the value of 
plaintiffs’ success cannot be measured. By preventing 


164 


the liquidation of the four institutions, the acquisi- 
tions not only saved FSLIC millions of dollars, they 
saved the jobs of hundreds of people and preserved 
important services for thousands of savers, home 
buyers, and other customers in the communities 
served by the insolvent thrifts. 

64. FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s contractual promises, 
for which plaintiffs provided substantial consideration 
and on which they detrimentally relied in fully 
performing their obligations under their agreements 
with FSLIC and FHLBB, entitled Statesman Bank to 
record supervisory goodwill, the $21 million capital 
credit, and the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture as credits to capital for 
purposes of defendants’ regulatory capital require- 
ments. These contractual promises also entitled 
Statesman Bank to report the $21 million capital 
credit as perpetual capital that need not be amor- 
tized. Plaintiffs’ contract rights arising out of the 
above-described agreements with FSLIC and 
FHLBB constitute property within the meaning of 
the Fifth Amendment to the United States 
Constitution. 


FIRREA 


65. FIRREA was signed into law by President 
Bush on August 9, 1989. Section 301 of FIRREA 
required the Director of OTS to promulgate, no later 
than November 7, 1989, final regulations establishing 
minimum capital requirements for federally insured 
thrifts. These minimum capital requirements became 
effective on December 7, 1989, and require federally 
insured thrifts to: 


165 


(a) maintain “tangible capital,” which excludes 
intangible assets including supervisory good- 
will, in an amount not less than 1.5 percent of 
the thrift’s assets; 

(b) maintain “core capital,” as defined in the 
statute, in an amount not less than 3 percent 
of the thrift’s assets; and, 

(c) maintain “risk-based capital” in an amount 
not materially less than that required by the 


Comptroller of the Currency for national 
banks. | 


66. FIRREA provides that “tangible capital” shall 
not include intangible assets and supervisory good- 
will, and that only limited amounts of “core capital” 
may be made up of supervisory goodwill. Prior to 
January 1, 1992, the amount of supervisory goodwill 
includable in core capital may not exceed 1.5 percent 
of total assets. Thereafter, the amount of supervisory 
goodwill (as a percentage of assets) includable in core 
capital declines each year, in accordance with the 
following schedule: 


January 1, 1992, to December 31, 1992 .... 1.0 percent 
January 1, 1993, to December 31, 1993 .... 0.75 percent 
January 1, 1994, to December 31, 1994 .... 0.375 percent 


For purposes of the minimum capital requirements, 
supervisory goodwill must be amortized over a period 
not to exceed 20 years. 

67. FIRREA does not abrogate contracts and 
forbearances of FHLBB and FSLIC, and indeed 
provides that all orders, resolutions, determinations 
and regulations of FHLBB and FSLIC shall continue 


166 


in effect and be enforceable against OTS and FDIC. 
FIRREA does not exclude FSLIC capital contribu- 
tions on the face amount of Subordinated Debenture 
from capital for purposes of determining compliance 
with any of the minimum capital requirements, at 
least where governed by contrary contractual provi- 
sions and forbearances. Nor does FIRREA require 
that supervisory goodwill be excluded from capital for 
purposes of determining compliance with minimum 
“risk-based” capital requirements. 

68. FIRREA also requires the Director of OTS to 
enforce the following restrictions against federally 
insured thrifts that do not meet the minimum capital 
standards promulgated under FIRREA: 


(a) After January 1, 1991, the Director must 
prohibit the asset growth of any federally 
insured thrift not in compliance with regula- 
tory capital standards, except that the Direc- 
tor may approve a “limited growth exception” 
upon specified conditions. In addition, the 
Director may restrict the asset growth of any 
such thrift at any time prior to January 1, 
1991. 

(b) As of January 8, 1990, the Director required 
thrifts that do not meet minimum capital 
standards under FIRREA to submit a plan 
(“capital plan”) that (1) addresses the thrift’s 
need for increased capital under FIRREA; (2) 
describes the manner in which the thrift will 
increase its capital to achieve the minimum 
capital standards; (3) specifies types and 
levels of activities the thrift will engage in; 
(4) requires any increase in assets to be 
accompanied by specified increases in tan- 
gible and risk-based capital; and, (5) is 


167 


acceptable to the Director. The Director must 
treat any material failure to comply with such 
a plan as an unsafe and unsound practice. 

(c) After January 1, 1991, the Director must 
require federally insured thrifts that do not 
meet minimum capital standards under FIR- 
REA to comply with a capital directive waich 
may include restrictions on the payment of 
dividends and on compensation, as the Direc- 
tor determines to be appropriate. FIRREA 
permits, but does not require, the Director to 
exempt thrifts from receipts of a capital 
directive in certain circumstances. 


69. The Director may impose other restrictions on 
federally insured thrifts that do not meet minimum 
capital standards promulgated under FIRREA: 


(a) The Director, in his discretion, may treat any 
thrift’s failure to maintain capital at minimum 
regulatory levels as an unsafe or unsound 
practice subjecting the thrift to a range of 
further actions by the Director, including, 
among other things, issuance of cease and 
desist orders, appointment of a conservator or 
receiver, and suspension or removal of of- 
ficers and directors. 

(b) Such thrifts also must notify the Director of 
the proposed addition of any individual to the 
board of directors or of the employment of 
any individual as a senior executive officer of 
the thrift 30 days before such addition or 
employment becomes effective, and the Direc- 
tor may before the end of that 30 days 
disapprove the addition or employment. 


70. FIRREA does not define “goodwill” or “super- 
visory goodwill.” 


168 
OTS CAPITAL REGULATION 


71. On November 8, 1989, OTS published minimum 
capital regulations (“OTS capital regulation”) pur- 
suant to FIRREA. The OTS capital regulation 
became effective on December 7, 1989. 

72. In accordance with FIRREA, the OTS capital 
regulation establishes a minimum “tangible” capital 
requirement of 1.5 percent of total assets and a 
minimum “core” capital requirement of 3 percent of 
total assets. Supervisory goodwill cannot be counted 
as “tangible” capital at all, and can be counted as 
“core” capital only in accordance with the phase-out 
schedule set forth in FIRREA (see paragraph 66 
above). 

73. FIRREA does not require that supervisory 
goodwill be excluded from capital for purposes of 
determining compliance with minimum “risk-based” 
capital requirements. Nonetheless, the OTS capital 
regulation permits the inclusion of supervisory good- 
will in capital for purposes of the minimum “risk- 
based” capital requirement only in accordance with 
the phase-out schedule set forth in FIRREA for 
determining compliance with the minimum “core” 
capital requirement (see paragraph 66 above). 

74. Under the OTS capital regulation, the $21 
million dollar capital credit, which the parties had 
agreed would be fully includable in Statesman Bank’s 
regulatory capital without reduction through amortiz- 
ation, is considered “qualified supervisory goodwill.” 
Therefore, it is excluded completely from Statesman 
Bank’s tangible capital, and only limited amounts may 
be used to satisfy Statesman Bank’s core and risk- 
based capital requirements. 

75. Under FIRREA and the OTS capital regula- 
tion, the $5 million face amount of the Subordinated 


169 


Debenture, which the parties had agreed would be 
fully includable in Statesman Bank’s regulatory 
capital, is considered “supplementary capital.” Under 
that designation only portions of that amount are 
includable to satisfy Statesman Bank’s risk-based 
capital requirement, and the amount is entirely 
excluded from Statesman Bank’s tangible and core 
capital requirements. 

76. Apart from the requirements and restrictions 
imposed by FIRREA on federally insured thrift 
institutions that fail to meet minimum capital require- 
ments, the OTS capital regulation mandates a variety 
of additional consequences. For example, in addition 
to submitting a capital plan for achieving compliance 
with the minimal capital requirements, a noncomply- 
ing thrift must, under the OTS capital regulation, 
certify that while the plan is under review by OTS, it 
will not (absent OTS approval) (1) grow beyond net 
interest credited, (2) make any capital distributions, 
or (3) act inconsistently with any other limitations on 
activities established by statute, regulation, or by 
OTS in supervisory guidance. If a plan is not 
approved by OTS, the submitting thrift “shall 
immediately and without any further action” refrain 
from increasing its assets beyond the amount held on 
the day it receives notice of the disapproval and 
comply with any other restrictions or limitations set 
forth in OTS’s written disapproval of the plan. The 
OTS capital regulation further provides that OTS 
may, “though enforcement proceedings or otherwise,” 
require any thrift that fails to meet any of its 
regulatory capital requirements to take one or more 
of the following corrective actions: 


(a) increase the amount of its regulatory capital 
to a specified level or levels; 


170 


(b) convene a meeting or meetings with the OTS 
supervision staff for the purpose of accom- 
plishing the objectives of this section; 

(c) reduce the rate of earnings that may be paid 
on savings accounts; 

(d) limit the receipt of deposits to those made to 
existing accounts; 

(e) cease or limit the issuance of new accounts of 
any or all classes or categories, except in 
exchange for existing accounts; 

(f) cease or limit lending or the making of a 
particular type or category of loan; 

(g) cease or limit the purchase of loans or the 
making of specified other investments; 

(h) limit operational expenditures to specified 
levels; 

(i) increase liquid assets and maintain such 
increased liquidity at specified levels; or 

(j) take such other action or actions as the 
Director may deem necessary or appropriate 
for the safety and soundness of the savings 
association, or the depositors or investors in 
the savings association. 


On or after January 1, 1991, these restrictions, “and 
any other restrictions the Director [of OTS] deter- 
mines appropriate,” may be included in a “capital 
directive” issued by the Director against a non- 
complying thrift. Finally, according to the OTS capital 
regulation, OTS must treat as an unsafe and unsound 
practice “any material failure by a savings association 
to comply with any plan, regulation, written agree- 
ment undertaken or order or directive issued to 
comply with the requirements of [the capital 
regulation].” 

77. In “Thrift Bulletin 36,” now effective, OTS 
specified that acceptance of a capital plan submitted 


171 


by a noncomplying thrift will be predicted on certain 
additional stringent conditions, including among 
others: 


(a) The management and the board of directors 
must execute an operating agreement which 
may provide, among other things, that if 
specific targets within the plan are not met or 
the thrift takes any action that does not 
comport with the plan, certain activities will 
be significantly restricted, a consent to merge 
agreement will be executed, or management 
and the board of directors will resign. 

(b) The board of directors and senior manage- 
ment for the thrift must file an amendment, 
acceptable to the OTS District Director, to 
the capital plan within 15 days following an 
event or circumstance that materially affects 
the validity of the capital plan or its 
underlying assumptions. At a minimum, the 
capital plan must be reviewed and revised as 
necessary on an annual basis. 

(c) The thrift must submit to the OTS District 
Director, within 20 days following the close of 
each calendar quarter, quarterly variance 
reports comparing actual capital to the tar- 
gets established in the capital plan. Failure to 
file such variance reports shall constitute an 
unsafe and unsound practice. Variances that 
the District Director deems to be material 
may be grounds for revoking acceptance to 
the capital plan. 


78. According to Thrift Bulletin 36, a thrift that 
fails to comply with any of its minimum capital 
requirements will be deemed to be in noncompliance 


172 


upon “adoption” of the OTS capital regulation, rather 
than upon the rejection of any capital plan submitted 
by the thrift. 

79. Thrift Bulletin 36 recognizes that, apart from 
the specific restrictions set forth in FIRREA and the 
OTS capital regulation, there are a significant number 
of related OTS regulations, rule, and policy state- 
ments that limit or restrict a variety of essential 
business activities, (e.g., payment of dividends, 
establishment of branches, receipt of Federal Home 
Loan Bank advances, investments in securities and 
real estate) by thrifts that do not meet minimum 
capital requirements. Thrift Bulletin 36 requires that 
thrifts “request and receive approval of specific, 
express exemptions” from the operation of such 
related regulations “as part of the capital plan to be 
deemed in capital compliance.” 


THE IMPACT OF FIRREA AND 
THE OTS REGULATION 


80. As of September 30, 1989, prior to the effective 
date of FIRREA and the OTS capital regulation, 
Statesman Bank’s regulatory capital was $53.2 
million—$15.8 million in excess of its required 
minimum of $37.4 million. As of that date, Statesman 
Bank reported supervisory goodwill and other intang- 
ible assets in the amount of $24,077,000, and fully 
included the OTS capital credit of $21 million and the 
$5 million face amount of the Subordinated Debenture 
in its regulatory capital. Statesman Bank does not 
satisfy the three new capital standards of FIRREA 
and the OTS capital regulation. As of December 31, 
1989, after the OTS capital regulation became 
effective, Statesman Bank reported tangible capital of 


173 


negative $2.8 million ($11.8 million less than the 
required level of $9 million); core capital of $6.8 
million ($11.2 million less than the required level of 
$18 million); and risk-based capital of $13.7 million 
($10 million less than the required level of $23.7 
million). If Statesman Bank were permitted to record 
as regulatory capital its remaining supervisory good- 
will, the $21 million capital credit, and the $5 million 
face amount of the Subordinated Debenture, it would 
have reported as of December 31, 1989, tangible 
capital of $23.2 million, core capital of $32.8 million, 
and risk-based capital of $36.5 million. Therefore, but 
for the provisions of FIRREA and the OTS capital 
regulation restricting the inclusion of these items in 
capital—restrictions that violate plaintiffs’ agree- 
ments with FSLIC and FHLBB—Statesman Bank 
would satisfy each of the three capital requirements. 
Accordingly, Statesman Bank is subject to restric- 
tions and penalties relating to FIRREA’s capital 
requirements solely because of the. repudiation of 
FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s agreements with plaintiffs. 
81. On January 5, 1990, Statesman Bank submitted 
a capital plan together with a proposed modification 
and extension of the March 1988 acquisition agree- 
ments. On March 13, 1990, OTS denied Statesman 
Bank’s capital plan. OTS notified Statesman Bank 
that it must comply with OTS’ policy statements set 
forth in bulletin RB3a-1, under which Statesman 
Bank would not be permitted to make any new loans 
or investments without the prior written approval of 
the District Director of OTS. After negotiations with 
the OTS District Director, Statesman Bank was 
granted only limited exceptions to those restrictions. 
82. Under the restrictions outlined above, -States- 
man Bank was subject to substantial competitive 
disadvantage in the marketplace for loans deposits. 


174 


Statesman Bank—on pain of the full range of 
regulatory economic sanctions, from cease and desist 
orders to receivership and liquidation—was subjected 
not only to OTS’s close supervisory scrutiny, but to 
its direction and control regarding virtually all of its 
business operations. Statesman Bank’s managerial 
discretion, which has produced a record of conserva- 
tive, sound and efficient management, was greatly 
circumscribed. In addition, the collateral con- 
sequences of the restrictions imposed by FIRREA 
and the OTS capital regulation were equally damag- 
ing. For example, Statesman Bank’s ability to attract 
and retain deposits, as well as its ability to borrow in 
capital markets, was impaired. The restrictions also 
reduced Statesman Bank’s ability to generate mort- 
gages and other loans, reduced its access to second- 
ary mortgage markets, and impaired its ability to 
generate profits. The asset growth restrictions re- 
duced Statesman Bank’s ability to obtain the advan- 
tages of growth available to thrifts, banks, and other 
financial institutions competing with it for the 
provision of mortgages and other loans and services 
in its market area. Moreover, the restrictions imposed 
under FIRREA and the OTS capital regulation 
impaired Statesman Bank’s ability to serve the needs 
of savers and home buyers in its market area. In 
these and countless other ways, Statesman Bank was 
denied the benefits of including its supervisory 
goodwill, the $21 million capital credit and the $5 
million face amount of the Subordinated Debenture in 
capital for purposes of complying with regulatory 
capital requirements—benefits that plaintiffs con- 
tracted for in their agreements with FSLIC and 
FHLBB. 

83. In essence confirming that the purpose of the 
Statesman Bank acquisition had been frustrated, on 
July 26, 1990, the Director, OTS concluded that 


175 


the [Statesman Bank] is in an unsafe and 
unsound condition to transact business due to 
having substantially insufficient capital in that 
the [Statesman Bank] has negative tangible 
capital, and ... the [Statesman Bank] has incurred 
or is likely to incur losses that will deplete all or 
substantia!ly all of its capital ... 


As a result, the Director, OTS appointed the 
Resolution Trust Corporation (“RTC”) as receiver for 
Statesman Bank. On that same date, OTS formed a 
new institution, Statesman Federal Savings Bank, to 
take over all of the assets and liabilities of Statesman 
Bank, and appointed RTC as conservator of that new 
institution. On July 27, 1990, RTC took possession of 
Statesman Bank. This action caused additional signifi- 
cant harm to the reputation of the SSHC, Statesman 
Group, and American Life in financial and other 
communities, and otherwise severely impaired their 
ability to conduct their operations in a profitable 
manner free from burdensome and costly government 
interference. 

84. Thus, the repudiation of FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s 
contractual promises frustrated the very purpose of 
the acquisition of Statesman Bank: the conversion of 
four failed thrifts into a sound, profitable institution 
serving the financial needs of the community. 

85. Prior to the appointment of a receiver for 
Statesman Bank by OTS, on June 6, 1990. plaintiffs 
filed a complaint in the United States District Court 
for the District of Columbia seeking, on contractual 
and constitutional grounds, to enjoin OTS and FDIC 
from abrogating their contractual commitments to 
include supervisory goodwill, the $21 million capital 
credit and the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture in regulatory capital, or, 


176 


alternatively, to have the transaction rescinded and 
otherwise restore the parties to the status quo ante. 
On August 15, 1990, plaintiffs dismissed this district 
court action without prejudice. 


COUNT I

regulatory capital requirements constitute a repudia-

described above, and effect a taking of plaintiffs’ 
property without just compensation, in violation of 
the Fifth Amendment to the United States 
Constitution.

Statesman Bank’s capital for purposes of satisfying 
the regulatory capital requirements constitute a 
repudiation and abrogation of contract rights, as 
described above, and effect a deprivation of plaintiffs 
property without due process of law, in violation of 
the Fifth Amendment to the United States 
Constitution.

minimum capital requirements constitute a repudia-

described above, for which plaintiffs are entitled to 
restitutionary relief. 


COUNT IV

credit and the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture in Statesman Bank’s regula- 
tory tangible capital constitute a repudiation and 
breach of FSLIC’s and FHLBB’s obligations under 
their contracts with plaintiffs, as ‘described above. 


COUNT V

95. The abrogation of the defendants’ contractual 
agreements by FIRREA and the OTS capital regula-

contract with FHLBB and FSLIC for the acquisition 
of the four thrifts, and deprives plaintiffs of the 
consideration for which they bargained. Plaintiffs are 
entitled to rescission of the transaction with defend- 
ants, and the return of plaintiffs’ investment of $21 


178 


million, plus compensation for all monies expended 
and costs incurred by plaintiffs, and for all benefits 
conferred upon defendants, through plaintiffs’ pur- 
chase, operation, and management of the four 
institutions. 


COUNT VI

enforcing against plaintiffs the provisions of FIRREA 
and the OTS capital regulation restricting the 
inclusion of the supervisory goodwill, the $21 million

Subordinated Debenture in Statesman Bank’s capital 
for purposes of satisfying regulatory capital 
requirements. 


PRAYER FOR RELIEF 


WHEREFORE, plaintiffs respectfully pray: 

(a) That the Court declare that the provisions of 
FIRREA and the OTS capital regulation restricting 
the inclusion of supervisory goodwill, the $21 million 
capital credit and the $5 million face amount of the 
Subordinated Debenture in capital for purposes of 
satisfying Statesman Bank’s regulatory capital re- 
quirements constitute a repudiation and abrogation of 
plaintiffs’ valid contract rights and effect a taking of 
plaintiffs’ property without just compensation and a 
deprivation of plaintiffs’ property without due process 
of law, in violation of the Fifth Amendment to the 
United States Constitution; 

(b) That the Court order that the transaction be 
rescinded and award plaintiffs monetary relief in the 


179

monies expended and costs incurred by plaintiffs, and 
for the value of all benefits conferred on defendants, 
through plaintiffs’ purchase, operation and manage- 
ment of the four thrifts, a total sum in excess of $30 

(c) That the Court grant plaintiffs their costs, 
interest, and attorney’s fees as allowed by law; and 

(d) That the Court grant such other and further 
relief as the law and evidence may justify and as the 
Court may deem just and proper.
